## THE OLD-JAVANESE <term>RĀMĀYAṆA<note>The Javanese version of the great Indian epic poem, which tells the story of Prince Rama's quest to rescue his wife Sita from the demon-king Ravana.</note></term> <term>KAKAWIN<note>A specific genre of Old Javanese narrative poetry written in traditional Indian meters.</note></term>

<meta>This page serves as the preface to a scholarly work, likely regarding the interaction between Indian and Southeast Asian cultures. It follows the title and front matter, establishing the theoretical framework of "acculturation."</meta>

# PREFACE

Acculturation, the assimilation of culture, is a word for a process and a problem as widely spread as it is fascinating.

It is used to denote those elements of a culture which are assimilated by another culture with which it comes into contact; it affects people, their thoughts, their habits, works of art and technique, administration and organisation. A considerable number of those engaged in Asian or African studies have spent part of their lives in Asia or in Africa and have played their role in this huge process, whether they had direct contact with the indigenous people or not, and whether they realised it or not, as much by their activities as by lack of them. On the surface the tide is against us at the moment, but future historians may investigate the strength of the undercurrents.

It is highly interesting to study a specimen of acculturation in the past and to investigate which aspects have been absorbed and which rejected; whether the whole thing has been accepted without question or whether the recipient has been as wise as the Indian <term>haṅsa</term> <note>A mythical swan or goose in Indian tradition, celebrated for its legendary ability to separate milk from water when they are mixed—a metaphor for spiritual or intellectual discernment.</note>, who absorbs the milk from a mixture of milk and water. The new shape of the adopted specimen and its modified function in the new surroundings, the influence it gains there — these are attractive and fascinating subjects to investigate.

> “How, transplanted to Cambodia, to Java, and in other countries, has Indian aesthetics given birth to Khmer art, Javanese art, and other Hindu arts of the Far East? This is one of the most delicate problems presented to archaeologists.” <note>original: "Comment, transplantée au Cambodge, à Java, et dans les autres pays, l’esthétique indienne a-t-elle donné naissance à l’art khmèr, à l’art javanais et aux autres arts hindous d’Extrême-Orient? C’est là un des problèmes les plus délicats qui s’offre aux archéologues."</note>

This quotation from G. Coedès’ <note>George Coedès (1886–1969) was a renowned French scholar of Southeast Asian history and archaeology.</note> “The Indianized States of Southeast Asia”, 1948, p. 423, used as a motto to H. G. Quaritch Wales’ book, “The Making of Greater India” <note>1</note>), was the kernel of a paper, recently read in the Royal Dutch Academy of Arts by the Leiden Professor Bosch on, “Local Genius and Old-Javanese Art” <note>2</note>). Dr. Bosch pointed to “the Indian art of the first eight centuries A.D.,

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s preface, moving from a general discussion of acculturation to the specific scholarly "gap" between Indian prototypes and Javanese artistic expressions. It introduces the Rāmāyaṇa as the primary subject of the study.</meta>

...known only fragmentarily due to the devastations mainly by Muslim invasions in Northern India on the one hand; on the other hand, the Hindu-Javanese art—of which the oldest monuments date from the eighth century and which, however much Indian in design and related to the mother-country, still has a distinctively deviating character of its own—no equivalent or prototype of which can be indicated in India. Between the two: the gap. It is part of the primary task of archaeological investigation to bridge this gap, by facts if possible, by hypotheses if necessary."

The investigation offered in the following pages concerns the field of literary art; still it may be found to contain some contribution to this subject.

One is fortunate if the subject of one's studies happens to be the *Rāmāyaṇa* <note>The Rāmāyaṇa is one of the two major ancient Indian epics, telling the story of Prince Rama's quest to rescue his wife Sita from the demon king Ravana.</note>, for this vast poem is not only held in very high esteem in the whole of South East Asia, but it is also easily accessible in translations in European languages. Even in an Indonesian connection, where generally knowledge of the Dutch language is an absolute necessity <note>original: "conditio sine qua non"</note>, unusual facilities present themselves. Stutterheim ³) and Zieseniss ⁴) wrote their books in German, Kats ⁵) and Beryl de Zoete × Walter Spies ⁶) in English. From Beryl de Zoete's book "Dance and Drama in Bali" we get a vivid picture of the important role that the *Rāmā*-story played some twenty years ago (and I am convinced, still plays): in recitations on special occasions; at cremations and at every important house festival. My own teacher <term>guru</term> <note>A spiritual teacher or mentor.</note> in the village in which I lived in Bali—who in his early youth had gone to the European School and also to a teacher <term>guru</term> of mysticism in the hills, several days' journey away—was in the habit of studying the *Rāmāyaṇa* with his best school friend, a Christian, "in order to become a better man." Indeed, it would be wrong if one assumed from Beryl de Zoete's book that outside Bali only Jogjakarta <note>Now commonly spelled Yogyakarta; a major city on the island of Java known as a center of traditional Javanese culture.</note> produced spectacular *Rāmāyaṇa* performances at the court (as seen in their illustrated brochure in English!). During my sojourn at this capital, I saw the whole story lasting ten complete nights, staged in the courtyard of a—

Textband, Tafelband. Der Indische Kulturkreis"</note>. Georg Müller Verlag, Munich 1924 (Thesis Leiden Univ.).
⁴) Alexander Zieseniss, *The Rāma Saga among the Malays, its Origin and Form* <note>original: "Die Rāma-Saga bei den Malaien, ihre Herkunft und Gestaltung"</note>. Hamburg, Friederichsen, De Gruyter & Co., m.b.H. 1928.

# I. INTRODUCTION

### § 1. *The literary genre of Kakawin.*

The so-called *Kakawin* <note>The word *Kakawin* literally means "the work of a *kawi*" (a poet or scholar). These are long narrative poems composed in Old Javanese using Sanskrit poetic meters.</note> (compositions of poets; poems) form such a conspicuous part of Old Javanese literature, that the language in which they were written was called *kawi* after them ⁹). They constitute a distinct group amidst the various literary genres, according to both the indigenous ¹⁰) and European views ¹¹); they appear, moreover, to be closely interrelated, as much in their common character and details ¹²) as

H. Kern, *Kawi Studies; Arjuna-Wiwāha, Canto I and II* <note>original: "Kawi-Studiën; Arjuna-Wiwāha, Zang I en II"</note>, 's Gravenhage, 1871.
A. B. Cohen Stuart, *Kawi Charters* <note>original: "Kawi-Oorkonden"</note>, Leiden, 1875.

¹⁰) Njoman Kadjĕng, Assistant Librarian of the Library of palm-leaf manuscripts <note>original: "lontar-boekerij"</note> Kirtya (= Foundation) Liefrinck-van der Tuuk, Singaradja, Bali, subdivides Group IV *Itihāsa* (epic works) according to Balinese principles into: a) *Parwa* <note>Prose versions of the Indian epics</note>, b) *Kakawin* (using Sanskrit meters), c) *Kidung* (using indigenous *Tĕngahan* or "intermediate" meters) and d) *Gaguritan* (using indigenous *Macapat* or "sung" meters) in *Preliminary Overview of the Literary Treasures present on Bali* <note>original: "Voorloopig Overzicht der op Bali aanwezige literatuurschat"</note>, Communications <note>original: "Mededeelingen"</note> K.L.v.d.T.I. Appendix I p. 19-40. In subsequent lists of newly acquired manuscripts in Communications 3 (1931), 4 (1935), 5 (1937), 6 (1939), 11 (1940) & 13 (1941), this system was continued.

¹¹) The Sanskrit scholar Dr. R. Friederich, in his above-mentioned Preliminary Report, distinguished roughly 3 groups only: 1) Sanskrit writings with Balinese commentary; 2) Kawi writings, including a) epic works which are holy for the people, like the *Rāmāyaṇa*, *Uttarakāṇḍa* & the *Parwas*; b) the less difficult Kawi poetry (sic), e.g. *Wiwāha*, *Bhārata-Yuddha*, etc. 3) Javanese-Balinese writings; it does not appear whether this division is his or his informants'.
A. C. Vreede, *Catalogue of Javanese and Madurese Manuscripts* <note>original: "Cat. Jav. Mad. MSS."</note>, University of Leiden Library 1892, distinguishes 9 groups; I. Epic poetry <note>original: "Heldendicht"</note>.
H. H. Juynboll, *Supplement to the Catalogue of Javanese and Madurese Manuscripts* <note>original: "Suppl. Cat. Jav. Mad. MSS."</note>, University of Leiden Library, 1907 & 1911 distinguishes 6 (or 8) groups; II Kakawin.

¹²) Lastly, A. Teeuw in his translation of *The Poem of Bhoma* <note>original: "Het Bhoma-kāwya"</note>, Groningen, 1946 p. 1, summarizes a century of investigations thus:
> “The connection as to outward appearance and contents of the various texts is so apparent that in studying those *Kakawins* one can hardly neglect one of them without incurring the risk of obtaining an incomplete or even a false view of the meaning of this kind of literature, and also of every text taken apart.”

It provides a detailed bibliographic overview of how both Balinese traditions and European scholars categorize these epic poems and related literary forms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the history and classification of Old Javanese Kakawin literature. in their fate ¹³); their number is considerable ¹⁴). On Bali they are still held in high esteem; recitation of them forming an integral part of cremation ceremonies and musical competitions ¹⁵), they are recited at homely gatherings ¹⁶), and studied by study-groups ¹⁷). They were the first literary works to command the attention of Westerners ¹⁸), they ¹⁹) and their *Modern*-Javanese counterparts ²⁰) were the earliest texts to be printed.

The progress made during more than a century of investigation is the more remarkable in view of the paucity of students devoted to Indonesian studies generally and to (Old- & *Modern*-)Javanese in particular, and to the intricate character of the difficulties they encountered; nevertheless it cannot be said to be satisfactory; prosodical <term>prosodical: Relating to prosody, the study of poetic meters and the rhythmic structure of verses.</term> studies must be said to have scarcely begun ²¹).

During the 19th. century Friederich printed the *Wṛtta-Sañcaya* <note>A manual on Sanskrit metrics written in Old Javanese verse.</note> from one manuscript, and edited the *Bhauma-Kāwya* <note>An epic poem concerning the death of the demon Bhoma at the hands of Krishna.</note> from two, not daring to draw more manuscripts into comparison or to give a translation.

<meta>This page continues the chronological survey of scholarship on Old Javanese literature, transitioning from the early 19th-century pioneers like Friederich to the more systematic linguistic and philological work of the late 19th and early 20th centuries, dominated by figures like Kern, Juynboll, and Brandes.</meta>

Cohen Stuart had a manuscript of the *Bhārata-Yuddha* <note>The "War of the Bharatas," a major Old Javanese epic poem composed in 1157 AD, based on the Indian Mahabharata.</note> from the Royal Asiatic Society lithographed <note>A printing process where text or images are etched onto a flat stone or metal plate.</note> $^{22)}$.

The famous Sanskritist <note>A scholar who specializes in the study of the Sanskrit language and its literature.</note> Kern was the first to give a new text with translation and annotations of the *Wṛtta-Sañcaya* <note>A 12th-century manual on Sanskrit poetic meters, written in Old Javanese verse.</note> $^{23)}$. He also made a tentative text + translation + copious annotations of 39 stanzas of the *Arjuna-Wiwāha* <note>The "Marriage of Arjuna," one of the most beloved Old Javanese poems, detailing the hero Arjuna's asceticism and victory over the demon Niwatakawaca.</note> $^{24)}$, of 28 verses of the *Bhārata-Yuddha* $^{25}$) and of 9 verses of the *Rāmāyaṇa* $^{26)}$.

The beginning of the 20th century saw Kern's monumental edition of the *Rāmāyaṇa Kakawin* <note>The Old Javanese version of the Ramayana epic, considered the "exemplary poem" (adi-kakawin) of the genre.</note> $^{27)}$, which was followed by Juynboll's Glossary to this text $^{28)}$; Kern's translation of cantos <term>cantos: The primary divisions of a long poem, known as 'sarga' in Sanskrit and Old Javanese.</term> I-VI $^{29}$) was gradually completed by Juynboll $^{30)}$. Gunning edited the *Bhārata-Yuddha* $^{31)}$, but did not publish the promised translation, which was only done 30 years later by Poerbatjaraka & myself <note>Referring to the author, Christian Hooykaas.</note> $^{32)}$. Brandes first intrigued students by printing the *Nāgarakṛtâgama* <note>A 14th-century eulogy and historical poem about King Hayam Wuruk of Majapahit, providing invaluable details on the administration and geography of the empire.</note> in Balinese script $^{33)}$,

---

In Kawi <note>The literary and administrative language of old Java.</note> text [Javanese characters] and Dutch translation, Leiden, Brill, 1875. Reprint in <note>original: "Verspreide Geschriften"</note> *Collected Writings* (VG). 9, 1920, p. 67-190 [Latin characters].
$^{24)}$ Kawi Studies, *Arjuna-Wiwāha*, Cantos <note>original: "Zang"</note> I and II in text and translation with notes and introduction. The Hague 1871, (not reprinted in Collected Writings; it is antiquated).
$^{25)}$ Canto XV of the *Bhārata-Yuddha* in Kawi, with translation and notes, *Bijdragen* <note>Referring to the journal "Bijdragen tot de Taal-, Land- en Volkenkunde," a leading academic journal for Southeast Asian studies.</note> 3/VIII = 20, 1873; reprinted in *Collected Writings* 9, 1920, p. 39-66.
$^{26)}$ <note>original: "Proeve uit het Oud-Javaansche Rāmāyaṇa"</note> A sample from the Old Javanese *Rāmāyaṇa*, *Bijdragen* on the occasion of the 6th International Congress of Orientalists at Leiden, 1883; reprinted in *Collected Writings* 9, 1920, 251-72.

...but then Kern gradually translated and explained this difficult text $^{34)}$, perhaps the most famous composition in Javanese and even in Indonesian literature <note>This refers to the *Nāgarakṛtāgama*, a 14th-century poem that serves as a primary historical source for the Majapahit Empire</note>. Poerbatjaraka <note>Raden Ngabehi Poerbatjaraka (1884–1964) was a self-taught Javanese scholar who became one of the most respected authorities on Old Javanese literature</note> edited and translated the *Arjuna-Wiwāha* <note>The Marriage of Arjuna</note> $^{35)}$, the *Smara-Dahana* <note>The Burning of the Love God</note> $^{36)}$, the *Nīti-śāstra* <note>A work on ethics and moral conduct</note> $^{37)}$ and recently the *Nirartha-Prakṛta* <note>A philosophical poem regarding religious liberation</note> $^{38)}$. Teeuw made a careful translation of the *Bhauma-Kāwya* <note>The Poem of the Son of Earth</note> $^{40)}$, nearly a century after its text had appeared in print, and edited and translated the *Hari-Wańśa* <note>The Lineage of Krishna</note> $^{41)}$.

The *Nāgarakṛtāgama* has been preserved in only one manuscript; fortunately, this has proved to be excellent, though not flawless. This text, relatively small as it is, has been the subject of a considerable amount of learned papers, thanks to the important facts which it gives in the field of history, geography, archaeology, religion, law, and so on.

Next comes the *Rāmāyaṇa*. A paper by Poerbatjaraka $^{42)}$ about its date provoked opposition $^{43)}$, whereupon its author "girded up his loins" and wrote a much larger article $^{44)}$, in which he not only added new material to support his view that the Old Javanese *Rāmāyaṇa* dates from the days of King Siṇḍok <note>A ruler of the Mataram Kingdom in the 10th century</note>, more than 1,000 years ago, but also three appendices: number 1 on interpolations <note>Sections of text added to the original by later scribes</note>, number 2 a complete list of all poetic meters, and number 3 an alphabetical list [and complete — ˘ ˘ <note>A notation for short/long syllable patterns in verse</note> scheme] of all meters used in the Old Javanese *Rāmāyaṇa* $^{45)}$.

## § 2. *The problem of interpolation remains unsolved.*

The subject which interests us here—the interpolations <note>sections of text added to a manuscript by later editors or scribes that were not part of the author's original work</note>—appears to be of real importance. Now, some time ago, a verse from a *Kakawin* was used to prove, for example, the relatively high antiquity of the shadow-play theatre $^{46)}$—but what is to be done when closer investigation refutes the authenticity of such a verse? $^{47)}$. Or—of more consequence for the text concerned—when evidence for the age of a work is found in a verse, the genuineness of which seems doubtful? $^{48)}$. How different the character of a *Kakawin* itself becomes, such as the *Arjuna-Wiwāha* <note>"Arjuna’s Wedding," a famous 11th-century poem</note> $^{49)}$ or the *Smara-Dahana* <note>"The Burning of the Love God," a 12th-century poem</note> $^{50)}$, after the scholar Poerbatjaraka has shorn them of some 20% of their "too luscious foliage and flowers"! Should we not perhaps suspect that the original nature of the work has been corrected too severely and that vital and essential parts have been removed? Moreover, what is one to think when the editor of the *Bhārata-Yuddha* <note>"The War of the Bharatas," an epic poem about the great war between the Pandavas and Kauravas</note> $^{51)}$ cuts away "only" 12%, while Poerbatjaraka rejects more than 20%? $^{52)}$. Or when he lays his hands even on the sacrosanct *Nāgarakṛtāgama* $^{53)}$, but there he finds the scholar Krom on the defensive $^{54)}$, not prepared to give way on even a single syllable? Or when the editor of the *Rāmāyaṇa*, Kern, puts only 4% of the Old Javanese *Rāmāyaṇa* between square brackets <note>signifying they are suspected additions</note> $^{55)}$, while its translator, Juynboll, marks 6% and Poerbatjaraka 8%? $^{56)}$.

What is one to think when a scholar of Kern’s fame declares (in 1873) that *all descriptions of love scenes, grief over the departure of a lover, etc., in the Bhārata-Yuddha are interpolated* $^{57)}$, which words...

<meta>This page continues the critique of how early scholars—specifically Hendrik Kern and his successors—handled sections of Old Javanese poems they believed were "interpolations" (later additions). ...are cited with approval by the editor of the poem, Gunning ⁵⁸), in 1903; repeated in the reprint in Kern's *Collected Works* <note>Original: *Verspreide Geschriften*</note> in 1920, and again quoted with agreement by Poerbatjaraka in 1931 ⁵⁹) when editing and translating (80% of) the *Smara-Dahana* <note>An Old Javanese poem titled "The Burning of the God of Love"</note>? I should say: to begin with, this is poetry, moreover poetry originating from a Hindu court which had no apparent reason to frown on love scenes and on the expression of sentiments in poetry. The matter seems worthy of investigation at closer quarters.

Actually the problem of interpolation <note>The insertion of material into a text by a later hand, which was not part of the original composition</note> has been dealt with seriously and repeatedly and at considerable length ⁶⁰). At one time "incredible thoughtlessness" was attributed to the supposed interpolator ⁶¹), at another time "silly artificiality" was the charge ⁶²); "difficulty" and "obscurity" are also often recurring objections ⁶³), --- but the primary cause of all the suspicion seems to be their erotic character. Kern, when introducing the problem of interpolation, as early as 1873, cut out 15 verses from the *Bhārata-Yuddha* <note>"The War of the Bharatas," an epic poem about the great war between the Pandavas and Kauravas</note> Canto XV in translation, gave his motives, and then went on with the generalisation quoted above, for which he added no arguments. Nowadays, more than 80 years later, in rather different times, one cannot easily disengage oneself from the impression, that in cutting out passages, specially more or less erotic ones, Kern notwithstanding the intellectual argument given in one instance, was here mainly acting as a gentleman of mid-Victorian days.

<summary>This page analyzes the scholarly tendency to label erotic passages in Old Javanese poetry as later "interpolations." The author suggests these edits were often based on the Victorian moral sensibilities of 19th-century Dutch scholars like Hendrik Kern rather than objective evidence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of scholars like Kern, Gunning, Juynboll, and Poerbatjaraka, who frequently removed passages from Old Javanese poems because they found them either too difficult or too erotic, labeling them as "interpolations" (later additions) without sufficient proof.</meta>

(unfor)tunately completely), cited those words and acted consistently in that direction, but failed to add one single word of justification for this procedure ⁶⁴), and Poerbatjaraka, for whom in later years "erotic" and "obscene" seem to have become synonymous in literature ⁶⁵), went still further ⁶⁶); we have seen to what extent.

For two reasons at least this view could be objected to; to begin with, neither Gunning nor Juynboll nor Poerbatjaraka dared to be consistent in cutting out erotic, difficult, and artificial passages. Where Kern had no objections at all in the <term>Old Javanese Ramayana</term> <note>abbreviated in text as OJR; one of the oldest and most famous epic poems in Javanese literature</note> <term>sarga</term> <note>a canto or chapter</note> XII, Juynboll and Poerbatjaraka disagree as to which stanzas are interpolated and which are genuine. Juynboll rejects the major part of <term>sarga</term> XXV (which is very difficult, if not impossible to translate), qualifying these stanzas as interpolated, but (fortunately) translated several other passages which have been constructed on the same principle and are just as artificial, e.g., XVI, 24—40.

Next Poerbatjaraka gradually evolves a hypothesis, according to which all the existing <term>Kakawins</term> <note>long narrative poems written in Old Javanese according to Indian poetic meters</note>, even all copies, without a single exception, should have been interpolated by the same poet, at a fairly late date of the Majapahit period <note>an empire that ruled much of the Indonesian archipelago from approximately 1293 to 1527</note>, but still before copies were introduced into Bali (and from there to Lombok) ⁶⁷). A priori unacceptable though it may be, this theory, devised to explain away the difficulty that not one single manuscript of any <term>Kakawin</term> has been found without these 'interpolations', has not yet been refuted.

## II. A WAY TO SOLUTION

### § 3. *Good Suggestions.*

However, warning voices have been heard. A sound critical scholar like Krom preferred attributing three 'silly' cantos to Prapañca—the poet of the <term>Nāgarakṛtāgama</term> <note>a famous 14th-century court poem detailing the Majapahit empire</note> himself—and not to a hypothetical later interpolator ⁶⁸).

<meta>This page continues the discussion of scholarly debates surrounding Old Javanese literature. It shifts focus to the work of Walther Aichele and Galestin, who challenged the tendency of earlier scholars to dismiss difficult or complex passages as later additions.</meta>

Aichele's elaborate studies on the <term>Shiva-hymn</term> <note>a devotional song dedicated to the god Shiva</note> of the <term>Arjuna-Wiwāha</term> **71)** <note>The Marriage of Arjuna, a 10th-century epic poem</note> and its initial verses **72)** will have made it clear to every student in this field, that some passages at least in the Old Javanese <term>Kakawins</term> <note>long narrative poems following Indian metrical patterns</note> were just meant to be difficult and ambiguous, highly artificial and perhaps even misleading.

Aichele even gave us "Fundamental Remarks for the Interpretation of <term>Kawi</term>" <note>the literary and poetic language of ancient Java</note> **73)**, not only in the paper of this name, but also in his "Outward Form of Kawi-Prosody", as early as 1926 **74)**. Here he pointed to the occurrence in the <term>Old Javanese Ramayana</term> <note>abbreviated as OJR in text</note> of Sanskrit <term>alaṅkāras</term> <note>stylistic "ornaments" or figures of speech</note> as these are dealt with in the Sanskrit textbooks on <term>prosody</term> <note>the study of poetic meters and forms</note> etc. He showed the existence of *Anuprāsa*, *Yamakas*, *Rūpaka*, *Utprekṣā*, *Apahnuti* or *Nirhnuti* & *Upamā* **75)**.

Galestin in his paper "Notes on the Arjuna-Wiwāha at <term>Candi Jago</term>" <note>original: "Tjaṇḍi Djago"; a 13th-century Buddhist-Hindu temple in East Java</note> **76)** — which mausoleum dates from around 1268 according to Krom in his "Introduction to Hindu-Javanese Art" **77)** — makes it more than probable that some stanzas branded in Poerbatjaraka's translation as being <term>interpolated</term> <note>later additions not belonging to the original text</note> cannot be dispensed with for a sound 'reading' (interpretation) of the text-in-sculpture; in this way the 'interpolation' is either forced back to a very early time (much earlier than Poerbatjaraka is generally inclined to assume), or points towards the genuineness of these passages.

> "It is undesirable to call '**interpolated**' <note>passages suspected of being added by later authors rather than the original poet</note> those passages in Old-Javanese ***Kakawins*** <note>long narrative poems following Indian metrical patterns</note> (,) which apparently are not related to the plot, before a comparative investigation has been made between the requirements to which these ***Kakawins*** must conform, especially as to the ***alaṅkāras*** <note>stylistic "ornaments" or figures of speech</note>, and those which are in force for the Indian ***kāvyas*** <note>classical Sanskrit court poetry</note>."

When editing and translating the ***Kakawin Nirartha-Prakṛta*** <note>The poem of "The Meaningless Nature" or "The Soul without Purpose"</note> in 1951 **79)**, Poerbatjaraka deemed it unnecessary to discuss the problem of interpolation again. For him this was solved. But Teeuw deliberately translated the complete ***Bhauma-Kāwya*** <note>The Poem of the Demon Bhauma</note> **80)**, edited, translated and annotated the complete ***Hari-Waṅśa*** <note>The Dynasty of Hari (Krishna)</note> **81)** in the hope of adding at least more material for the ultimate solution of the problem, which he too considers to be not yet definitely settled.

In 1934, as No. 1 of the Greater India Studies, there appeared a useful compilation by Himansu Bhusan Sarkar: "***Indian Influences on the Literature of Java and Bali***" **82)**. Dutch students were not very enthusiastic about it **83)**, failing however to substitute anything better, failing also fully to profit from it: for his chapter on the ***Rāmāyaṇa*** the author mentions sarga <note>chapter or canto</note> II verse 19 — I quote Sarkar —

> "When the poet, like the author of the ***Bhaṭṭikāvyam*** <note>a 7th-century Sanskrit poem designed to illustrate the rules of grammar and poetry</note>, says: — 'There was no water-place which was without lotuses. There were no lotuses which were not full of bees, and the bees were buzzing. There were no bees which would allow their songs to go unheard', and so on."

In 1936, another Indian scholar, Manomohan Ghosh, wrote an article in the *Journal of the Greater India Society* under the heading: — "***On the Source of the Old-Javanese Rāmāyaṇa Kakawin***" **84)**. He had been struck by three passages, first that quoted by Sarkar (OJR <note>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</note> II. 19 = BhK <note>Bhaṭṭikāvyam</note> II. 19), secondly by the description of **Rāvaṇa's** <note>the demon-king antagonist of the Rāmāyaṇa</note> appearance in the guise of an ascetic (OJR V. 65—67 = BhK V. 61—4), thirdly by that of the conversation between **Sītā** <note>the wife of the hero Rāma</note> and **Rāvaṇa** (OJR V. 69—73 = BhK V. 65—9).

<meta>This page continues the scholarly investigation into the origins of the Old Javanese Ramayana. It specifically details the arguments made by the Indian scholar Manomohan Ghosh in 1934, who proposed that the Javanese poem was not a direct translation of Valmiki’s classical Sanskrit Ramayana, but was instead closely modeled on the Bhaṭṭi-kāvya, a different Sanskrit poem designed to teach grammar.</meta>

through the media of two different languages, convinced me that this similarity might not be quite accidental and that Yogiśvara <note>Traditionally regarded as the author of the Old Javanese Ramayana, though the name may be a title rather than a personal name</note>, the author of the ***Kakawin*** <note>A long narrative poem in Old Javanese written in meters derived from Sanskrit</note>, was most probably acquainted with the ***Bhaṭṭi-kāvya*** <note>A 7th-century Sanskrit poem that tells the story of Rama while simultaneously illustrating rules of Sanskrit grammar</note> itself.”

After the following three <term>ślokas</term> <note>Sanskrit verse units, typically consisting of two lines or four quarters</note> Ghosh speaks about expressions [in the Old Javanese Ramayana] “Marvellously similar to those in the corresponding passages of the ***Bhaṭṭi-kāvya***”, and goes on to say: — “One can easily notice that expressions . . . in *Bhaṭṭi* . . . have been almost literally translated in the ***Kakawin***, the author of which appears to have taken as much from the *Bhaṭṭi* as he could easily assimilate in his own work. A literal versified translation is surely an almost impossible task. Thus it may be assumed that Yogiśvara while writing the ***Kakawin*** had the ***Bhaṭṭi-kāvya*** before him and adopted the theme of the latter in its details and, as often as possible, number of expressions as well.”

Finally after comparing the last 5 <term>ślokas</term> mentioned, he concludes : — “Now the *Bhaṭṭi*-strophes, quoted above, show beyond doubt that the author of the ***Kakawin*** has deliberately copied the contents of the *Bhaṭṭi* and at times made a literal translation of some of the expressions. Due to the fact that Sanskrit strophes of *Bhaṭṭi* had sometimes to be expanded and sometimes original strophes were composed, the number of strophes in the ***Kakawin*** and the *Bhaṭṭi* is not identical. The former work contains 2771 strophes and the latter only 1624 <note>The scholar Dr. Poerbatjaraka suggested that some sections were added later (interpolated) into the Javanese version, which would explain why it is so much longer. See *Indian Influences*, page 173</note>.”

Ghosh ends by saying : — “An examination of cantos VI-XX of the ***Kakawin*** [in those days Juynboll’s translation had not proceeded further than that] reveals that unlike the first five ones their subject matter does not correspond canto by canto to that of the *Bhaṭṭi*, and moreover the ***Kakawin*** has 26 cantos while the *Bhaṭṭi* has only 22.”

> “Thus we can conclude finally that the ***OJR*** <note>Old Javanese Ramayana</note> is partially a translation and partially an adaptation of the ***BhK*** <note>Bhaṭṭi-kāvya</note> and has nothing to do with the *Rāmāyaṇa* of Vālmīki <note>The most famous and original version of the Rama story in Sanskrit</note>, or its conjectured extinct translation in the Old Javanese <note>For further theories regarding the origin of this poem, see *Indian Influences*, page 174</note>.”

These conclusions, based upon comparison of 9 <term>ślokas</term> and some pages of extracts, were certainly somewhat rash and were insufficient proof, more guess than proof — to a great extent however, they prove to hit the nail on the head. <meta>This page continues the academic discussion regarding the source material of the Old Javanese Ramayana. I undertook the task of making a complete translation, in the making of which I profited greatly from the encouragement and generous help given to me by my colleague C. A. Rylands. Only one-third (see note 85) of the ***Bhaṭṭikāvya*** <note>original: "BhK"; a 7th-century Sanskrit poem that narrates the life of Rama while illustrating grammatical rules</note> had been translated—545 out of a total of 1625 stanzas—to which recently 67 stanzas (4%) have been added by professor John Brough (see note 86). The ***Old Javanese Ramayana*** <note>original: "OJR"; the oldest and most significant epic poem in Old Javanese literature</note> has been completely rendered into Dutch (see note 87), with the exception of only a few minor passages (see note 88), together being not more than 161 stanzas out of a total of 2774. The evidence thus gained shows Ghosh’s conclusions to be justified.

## *§ 4. Dr. Bulcke’s results.*

When the making of my translation was nearly completed, it was interrupted for a considerable length of time by my visit to India in 1953, where I had the great advantage of meeting Dr. Camille Bulcke <term>S.J.</term> <note>Society of Jesus; Dr. Bulcke was a Jesuit priest and a renowned scholar of Hindi and Sanskrit</note>, the author of several recent articles on various aspects of the ***Ramayana*** <note>The Great Epic of Rama, one of the two major Sanskrit epics of ancient India</note> (see note 89). He proved to have written a thesis for the degree of Ph. D., University of Allahabad, in 1950, which had been printed there in Hindī as ***The Story of Rama*** <note>original: "Rāma-Kathā"</note>. He has compared the complete ***Old Javanese Ramayana*** in its Dutch translation with the ***Bhaṭṭikāvya***, and he has established the close relation between the two, which goes as far as (roughly) the first two thirds. He kindly sent me the English translation of his section 224, page 182, on the ***Bhaṭṭikāvya*** or ***The Death of Ravana*** <note>original: "Rāvaṇa-vadha", an alternative title for the poem</note> (500—650 A.D.) and section 299, page 232, on the ***Old Rama-story of Indonesia***. His conclusions, based upon investigation of the complete texts, state the dependence of the ***Old Javanese Ramayana*** on the ***Bhaṭṭikāvya***, and consist of an enumeration of peculiarities of the ***Bhaṭṭikāvya*** in comparison with other ***Ramayana*** texts and in common with the ***Old Javanese Ramayana***.

Bulcke’s field is a very vast one and a comparison between the details of the ***Old Javanese Ramayana*** and the ***Bhaṭṭikāvya*** such as we need for our purpose was outside his

Before proceeding, however, attention must be drawn to the fact that already Poerbatjaraka <note>a renowned Indonesian scholar of Javanese literature</note> had pleaded for recognition of Yogîśwara's <note>the traditional name attributed to the author of the Old Javanese Ramayana</note> great knowledge of Sanskrit (Kern <note>Hendrik Kern, a pioneering Dutch scholar of Oriental studies</note> had denied him any knowledge of it). Following my objections to Poerbatjaraka's theory of interpolation <note>the insertion of later material into an original text</note>, it seems only decent to point to his keen gift of observation and to his sound argumentation on the first occasion that offers itself.

### **III. DETAILED COMPARISON OF EXAMPLE AND IMITATION**

#### **§ 5. *The Bhaṭṭi-Kāvya. Form.***

a) The handbooks on Indian Literature—Winternitz, Keith, and Dasgupta + De—do not fail to instruct us concerning the age and character of this remarkable poem. The author, Bhaṭṭi, used the well-known story of the ***Ramayana*** <note>original: "Rāmāyaṇa"; the Great Epic of Rama</note> (***The Killing of Ravana*** <note>original: "Rāvaṇa-vadha"</note>) in order to demonstrate with his verses the difficulties of grammar and the possibilities of literary embellishment (***alaṅkāra*** <note>literary ornaments or figures of speech</note>). By taking this familiar theme, he made it definitely less difficult for his pupils—readers are here not so much the category concerned—to learn the whole subject by heart, as the thread of the story was already known to them. He managed to make verses, though he was mainly concerned with special cases of—and exceptions to—Pāṇini's <note>the ancient scholar who formulated the definitive rules of Sanskrit grammar</note> rules on the aorist <note>a past tense</note>, future, etc. For thirteen centuries at least, this struggle between poet and teacher has enjoyed wide-spread attention throughout India; no less than thirteen commentaries have gained a reputation in the course of those centuries, and the text is still studied and printed even today. Bhaṭṭi's ***The Killing of Ravana*** is not only a ***kāvya*** <note>a style of ornate, courtly poetry</note>, one of many, but is numbered among the few ***mahā-kāvya*** <note>great courtly epics</note> of Indian literature; it became popularly known under the name ***Bhaṭṭi-kāvya***, just as Māgha's ***The Killing of Shishupala*** <note>original: "Śiśupāla-vadha"</note> became known as the ***Magha-kavya***, and apparently as Candragomin's grammar (***vyākaraṇa***) became the ***Candra-grammar***, known as the ***Moonlight*** <note>original: "Canda-Kiraṇa"</note> in Indonesia ⁹⁰).

<meta>This page continues the formal analysis of the Bhaṭṭikāvya, focusing on its poetic structure and comparing its length to other major Sanskrit works to contextualize its relationship with the Old Javanese version.</meta>

Modern Indian scholars, such as Dasgupta and De <note>Surendranath Dasgupta and Sushil Kumar De, authors of a definitive history of Sanskrit literature</note>, have expressed objections to granting much poetical merit to Bhaṭṭi’s great achievement, taking a more critical stance than Keith did in 1920 <note>Arthur Berriedale Keith, a famous Scottish scholar of Sanskrit</note>. It has occurred to me that no one has yet criticized the way Bhaṭṭi skimps on his concluding chapters. In these sections, he mentions major episodes only to treat them very briefly. For example, in the second half of chapter XIV, verse 1, he writes: *vimohya māyā-maya-Rāma-murdhnā, Sītām anīkaṁ prajighāya yoddhum* <note>original Sanskrit: "Having deluded Sita with a magical, illusory head of Rama, [Ravana] sent forth his army to battle."</note>. This refers only briefly to an episode that both Vālmīki <note>the legendary author of the original Sanskrit Ramayana</note> in Book VI, chapters 31–33, and Yogîśwara <note>the traditional name for the author of the Old Javanese Ramayana</note> in Chapter XVII, verses 1–60, felt it necessary to describe at great length. Bhaṭṭi’s condensed method here does not align well with the instructions of Daṇḍin <note>a famous 7th-century poet and grammarian</note> in his *Mirror of Poetry* (*Kāvyādarśa*) I. 18, which states that a <term>mahā-kāvyam<gloss>a "great court epic," a specific genre of long-form classical Indian poetry</gloss></term> should be *a-saṅkṣiptam* <note>original Sanskrit: "not condensed" or "not too brief"</note>.

b) There is nothing unusual, however, in Bhaṭṭi’s decision to divide his poem, *The Killing of Ravana* (*Rāvaṇa-vadha*), into 22 <term>sargas<gloss>chapters or cantos within a long poem</gloss></term>. This follows Daṇḍin’s instructions in the *Mirror of Poetry* I. 18 regarding the Great Epic: *sargair an-ati-vistīrṇaiḥ (upetam)*, meaning "it should be composed of chapters that are not excessively long."

c) The number of verses in each chapter varies considerably across different famous epics:

g) Whereas Daṇḍin's *Mirror of Poetry* (*Kāvyâdarśa*) I. 14 prescribes for a major epic: — *āśīr-namaskriyā vastu-nirdeśo vâpi tan-mukham* <note>original Sanskrit: "A blessing, a salutation, or the naming of the subject matter serves as its opening"</note> (that the beginning of a classical poem should consist of a prayer/wish/benediction, worship/adoration or an introduction to the topic) our *Killing of Ravana* begins with Rāma's father and the preparations to obtain male offspring, resulting in Rāma's birth. Yogīśwara here followed his example faithfully. <meta>This page continues the technical comparison of poetic structures, specifically discussing the use of introductory blessings and common verse meters in the Bhaṭṭi-kāvya and the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa.</meta>

have their initial <term>maṅgala<gloss>a formal blessing or prayer at the beginning of a poem to ensure its successful completion</gloss></term>, and an intricate one too! thereby proving that their poets were not inspired by the <term>Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>the "Life of Rama," an ancient Indian epic</gloss></term> of Yogīśwara <note>traditionally considered the author of the Old Javanese version of the epic</note> only, but must have been conversant either with Indian poetics or with Indian <term>kāvyas<gloss>highly stylized and sophisticated courtly epic poems</gloss></term>, or possibly with both of them.

h) Notwithstanding the numerous changes in metre, there remains a predilection for the <term>Vaktra, Anuṣṭubh, or Śloka<gloss>these are names for the most common, traditional eight-syllable verse forms used in Sanskrit literature</gloss></term>, making up three-quarters of the stanzas in this textbook. In § 7 e) we shall see how Yogīśwara chose just the opposite order.

Note: The value of statistical methods largely depends upon the materials chosen and the way in which the dates have been handled. The treatment of the data I think is so simple, that no mishaps can have occurred here; the material, however, was not *chosen* but *found*, to be perfectly frank, in Kühnau’s *Metrical Collections from Stenzler’s Literary Estate* <note>original German: "Metrische Sammlungen aus Stenzler's Nachlass"</note>, published in the *Journal of the German Oriental Society* <note>original German: "Zeitschrift der Deutschen Morgenländischen Gesellschaft" or ZDMG</note> 44, 1890, pages 1—82. They had only to be transposed, and (being so easily accessible) saved a considerable amount of time. I doubt whether a real choice would have been any more convincing or more worth while as to the results obtained. As it happened I found just what I needed.

### § 6. *The Poem of Bhaṭṭi* (*Bhaṭṭi-kāvya*): *Contents*.

Whereas the outward appearance of the *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya* (BhK) had to be compared to the other Sanskrit *kāvyas*, and incidentally with the Old Javanese *Rāmāyaṇa* (OJR) — a subject reserved for the next section — the peculiarities of its contents cannot be compared here with the hundreds of other versions of the *Rāmāyaṇa*. One of the two most outstanding versions must be chosen, and since the version by Tulsi Dās <note>a 16th-century poet who wrote the popular Hindi version of the Rama story</note> is far too modern, Vālmīki's *Rāmāyaṇa* has been taken, as if it were the prototype or standard. In fact it has attained an authoritative standard and is easily obtainable in print, in Sanskrit and in English and in Jacobi's German extracts.

Though in this section we should only be concerned with the contents of the *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya*, in practice it is more satisfactory to discuss at the same time the corresponding peculiarities or deviations in the Old Javanese *Rāmāyaṇa*. The differences between the *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya* and the Old Javanese *Rāmāyaṇa* will be put together in § 8. As mentioned in § 4, Dr. Bulcke has already pointed in his book to most of the issues raised here.

a) *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya* I. 3. Daśaratha <note>the King of Ayodhya and father of Rama</note> is mentioned as being a worshipper of the god Śiva. So also in the Old Javanese *Rāmāyaṇa* I. 7. Dr. Poerbatjaraka <note>a famous Indonesian scholar of Javanese literature</note> in the work cited <note>original Latin abbreviation: "o. c." for "opere citato"</note> on pages 169—71 lays due stress on the "almost fanatical" Śivaitic spirit <note>referring to the worship of the Hindu god Śiva</note> breathed throughout the whole Old Javanese *Rāmāyaṇa*, which is demonstrated at every possible occasion. This Śivaitic...

tendency in the <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya<gloss>a 7th-century Sanskrit poem designed to illustrate grammar rules while telling the story of Rama</gloss></term> might perhaps be one of the reasons why Yogiśwara <note>the traditionally attributed author of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</note> felt inclined to take just this <term>mahākāvya<gloss>a "great courtly epic," a highly sophisticated form of long-form poetry</gloss></term> as his example; its character as a textbook mainly of Sanskrit grammar is not likely to have inspired him.

b) <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya<gloss>abbreviated as BhK</gloss></term> I. 13. At the sacrifice of Daśaratha <note>the father of Rama</note> no god appears, but Daśaratha's queens eat the remains of the burnt offering. The <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>abbreviated as OJR</gloss></term> I. 31 says exactly the same thing, but Kern <note>Hendrik Kern, a pioneering Dutch scholar of Indonesian studies</note> made a mistake in his translation ⁹¹): "The other great Sages <note>original Dutch: "De overige groote Ṛṣi's"</note> who paid homage with a completely glorious sacrifice of that which promotes health through fragrance and flavor; they too were fed by the King's wives." Some years ago a Balinese friend, I Wayan Bhadra—author of the interesting booklet on *Mabasan* ⁹²) or the *Study of Old-Javanese on the Island of Bali*—wrote me a letter about this passage, the translation of which he and his friends questioned. Their proposed translation was "that the remains of the food, presented to the sages, but not eaten by them, were distributed among the queens, who promptly conceived." It is interesting to read how Wayan Bhadra adds, by way of support to his boldness in modifying Kern: — "Just as is still being practiced by us in similar circumstances."

c) <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> II. 21. Instead of the <term>mantras<gloss>sacred chants or spells</gloss></term> named Balā and Atibalā as found in the original Indian epic by Vālmīki, the personified sciences Jayā and Wijayā are mentioned. The <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> II. 22. adds two more mantras, taught to Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa: Atidurjayā and Jayantī.

d) <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> II. 43. Only Rāma's marriage is mentioned. The same occurs in the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> II. 63.

e) <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> IV. 34—V. 3. Both Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa kill Khara, Dūṣaṇa, and the (14,000) <term>rākṣasas<gloss>demons or monstrous beings in Hindu mythology</gloss></term>. The same occurs in the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> IV. 67—V. 6.

f) <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> VI. 41. Rāma meets Jaṭāyu <note>the divine vulture who tried to rescue Sita</note> for the first time AFTER the stealing of Sītā. The <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> VI. 65 follows this.

g) <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> XI. 3-33. The love-play of the demons. Bhaṭṭi includes this passage, which is not essential to the plot of the poem, in order to demonstrate <term>mādhurya<gloss>a poetic quality of sweetness, charm, or aesthetic beauty</gloss></term>. Yogiśwara XII. 3-45 follows his example faithfully. This passage is a key-stone, for the finding of which I was studying the <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term>, since the scholars Juynboll and Poerbatjaraka consider it with equal horror, declaring it to be untranslatable and unworthy of their poet. Moreover, Poerbatjaraka cuts out some stanzas which Juynboll keeps, accepting on the other hand stanzas which Juynboll has rejected, with

h) <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> XII. 1: <term>Wibhīṣaṇa<gloss>the younger brother of the demon king Rāwaṇa who defects to Rama's side</gloss></term> honours and consults his mother; this matches the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>abbreviated as OJR</gloss></term> XIII. 3-15.

l) The fate of some <term>rākṣasas<gloss>demons or celestial spirits in Hindu mythology</gloss></term> in the <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> and the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> deviates from that in <term>Vālmīki<gloss>the legendary author of the original Sanskrit Rāmāyaṇa</gloss></term>; see Appendix IV.

m) Both the <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> and the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> stop with the <term>Yuddha-kāṇḍa<gloss>the "Book of War," the sixth book of the epic</gloss></term> in <term>Vālmīki</term>.

### § 7. *Old-Javanese Rāmāyaṇa. Form.*

a) Dr. Poerbatjaraka <note>a pioneering Javanese scholar of Sanskrit and Old Javanese literature</note> adds considerably to the value of his study "The <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term>" in the <term>Journal of Indonesian Linguistics, Geography, and Ethnology<gloss>TBG 72, 1932, p. 151—214</gloss></term> by adding his three appendices mentioned previously.

Kern’s <note>Hendrik Kern, the first scholar to edit and translate the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</note> edition is not easily available, and when obtained is partially difficult to follow; the same must be admitted for the Dutch translation, scattered over 17 issues of a learned paper. For these reasons a table of contents has been made and added to this study as Appendix I. Not only the contents from the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> have been extracted, but also the corresponding <term>sargas<gloss>chapters or cantos of a poem</gloss></term> I-XIII of the <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> have been taken into account; where they completely failed us (i.e. where the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> added stanzas to its prototype), the failing or added passage has been put into italics; where the connection was loose, the numbers only have been put into italics. For the last part, a comparison with <term>Vālmīki</term> has been added.

A second table had to be added, but constructed the other way round, first giving the complete number of stanzas in <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> I-XIII, followed by the corresponding numbers in the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term>. It will not be necessary to stress for those conversant with the peculiarities of <term>Kāvyas<gloss>highly stylized Sanskrit courtly poems</gloss></term> & <term>kakawins<gloss>Old Javanese poems written in Indian meters</gloss></term>, that this close comparison of a textbook/<term>mahākāvya<gloss>a "great poem" or epic</gloss></term> in Sanskrit with (we-do-not-yet-know-what-kind-of) a <term>kakawin</term> in <term>Old Javanese</term>, is rather a hazardous undertaking. Details may have been overlooked or, on the other hand, have been stressed too much. It is partly meant to be a means of orientation, to form an indispensable basis for discussion & further research, but it will appear that more may be deduced from it. Here, reference to <term>Vālmīki</term> could also easily be added.

The first mentioned table could also be used to give a clear idea concerning the metres used in the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>abbreviated as OJR</gloss></term>, the <term>rasa<gloss>the "aesthetic flavor" or emotional essence of a poetic work</gloss></term> & <term>bhāva<gloss>the psychological state or mood conveyed to the audience</gloss></term> of the passages (referencing <term>Daṇḍin<gloss>a 7th-century Sanskrit grammarian and poetic theorist</gloss></term> I. 18), the frequency of variation and the unusual variety of metres, which will be discussed in this section. Those metres of which the names are mentioned in this text have been put into italics.

b) The <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> acceptedly consists of 26 <term>cantos<gloss>sargas; the primary divisional units of a Sanskrit-style epic</gloss></term>, only 4 more than the <term>Naiṣadhīya<gloss>the Naiṣadhīya Carita, a masterpiece of Sanskrit courtly poetry</gloss></term>, which contains roughly as many verses, or the <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya<gloss>abbreviated as BhK; the 7th-century model for the Javanese poem</gloss></term> which deals with the same story and which served as the example. To begin with, <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> canto IX = <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> cantos IX + X; <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> XII = <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> XIII + XIV; <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> XIII = <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> XV + XVI. Moreover, <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> XVII + XVIII — XIX. 11 are an addition to the <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term>. The battle until <term>Rāwaṇa’s<gloss>the demon king of Lanka</gloss></term> death is narrated in <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> XIV—XVII, 4 cantos out of 22; in the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> XIX—XXIII = 5 out of 26 — which is the same proportion. The number of stanzas used for the battle in <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> is 390/1625 = 24%; in <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> 645/2774 = 23% — which is also the same proportion. But the way of dealing with the story’s conclusion is not in keeping: Bhaṭṭi hurries through it in 5 exceptionally short cantos, altogether 167/1625 stanzas, 10% of the whole poem; the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> spends not completely 3 cantos on the <term>dénouement<gloss>the final resolution of the plot</gloss></term> (<term>Rāwaṇa</term> dies in canto XXIV. 27), 402/2774 stanzas, which seems to be nearly 15% of the whole poem, but is actually a little bit more, as in the final canto the poet uses several very long <term>Daṇḍaka-stanzas<gloss>a specific class of Sanskrit metre with very long lines, often used for dramatic or descriptive intensity</gloss></term>. At the moment no more need be said about the division into cantos.

c) The number of stanzas in the single cantos of the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> offers nothing remarkable; 62. 78. 86. 76. **89. 203.** 113. **215.** (93. 72.) 96. 65. (97. 70.) (69. 47.) 138. 52. 131. 80. **248.** 89. 85. 260. 117. 52.⁹³)

d) <term>Dr. Poerbatjaraka<gloss>a pioneering Javanese scholar of Old Javanese literature (1884–1964)</gloss></term> <note>original: "o. c. p."</note> in the work cited, pages 180—5, is much worried about the fact that not all cantos end as they should, according to the <term>kāvya-principle<gloss>the formal rules of Sanskrit courtly poetry, which dictate that a chapter should end with a change in metre</gloss></term>, with one or two final stanzas of a different metre. He is quite right, but the discovery that <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term> is the example for the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> can put him perfectly at ease again.

To begin with, canto V seems to consist only of 1—89 <term>Rajanī<gloss>a specific poetic metre</gloss></term>,

---

⁹³) It will appear from the next section d, that probably 27 stanzas, now forming the beginning of canto VI, should be considered to be the end of canto V; then instead of 89 and 203 we should read 116 and 176. Moreover in canto VIII one single stanza is lacking in <term>Kern’s<gloss>Hendrik Kern, the Dutch scholar who first edited the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa in 1900</gloss></term> edition; if this stanza 135 is not to be found in any manuscript, then we have to take the total as 214. The same applies to canto XXI, where stanzas 93-7 are missing, reducing the total from 248 to 243. None of these alterations are of any consequence — but see Appendix V. More important is the possibility that cantos, which we find as entities, should be taken together, (IX + X, XIII + XIV, XV + XVI at least according to section d <term>infra<gloss>below</gloss></term>) reducing the total of the cantos from 26 to 23 or even less — but even this major operation is not necessarily essential.

without any final stanzas. The <term>Various Readings<gloss>Latin original: "Variae Lectiones"; a scholarly list of differences found in various versions of a manuscript</gloss></term> to Kern’s edition shed no light here. Dr. P. Voorhoeve, <term>assistant to the curator of the Warner Legation<gloss>Latin original: "adiutor interpretis Legati Warneriani"; a specific academic position within the Leiden University Library’s oriental collection</gloss></term>, Leiden University Library, was kind enough to examine 5 more Balinese manuscripts of the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>abbreviated as OJR</gloss></term> in this respect, but all of them ended <term>canto<gloss>sarga</gloss></term> V at exactly the same stanza, with exactly the same words in exactly the same metre as in Kern’s edition. 

But — as can be seen from the concordance — the end of the narrative in <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya<gloss>abbreviated as BhK; the Sanskrit model for the Javanese text</gloss></term> V coincides with <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> VI. 27 <term>Puṣpitâgrā<gloss>a Sanskrit poetic meter characterized by a "flowering" or varied structure at the end of the lines</gloss></term>, a metre which Yogīśwara <note>The traditional name attributed to the author of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa.</note> also used as the conclusion for the preceding <term>canto</term> IV (in I-III he uses <term>Mālinī<gloss>a meter of fifteen syllables, often used for descriptions of nature</gloss></term>). In § 5 d it has been pointed out that <term>Puṣpitâgrā</term> is the concluding verse in <term>Bhaṭṭikāvya</term>; actually it is a feature common to all the <term>courtly epics<gloss>kāvya</gloss></term> that have been used for comparison. 

So it seems not too dubious to surmise, that at some early date in the copying of the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> an error has crept in here — apparently there is no reason for a deliberate reshaping of the division into <term>cantos</term> — and has maintained itself in many a copy. It remains to be investigated, whether indeed all existing manuscripts of the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> — I expect there are many scores of them in Bali — reveal the same error; the placing of the rather easily-found words, <note>Sanskrit: "iti pañcamah sargga"</note> *“thus ends the fifth canto”*, might prove to be the first and handiest <term>shibboleth<gloss>a distinguishing test or marker used to categorize groups</gloss></term> in further <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> investigations.

There might be still some hesitation in accepting the possibility of this error in the venerated and generally well-preserved text of the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term>, and it might not be superfluous to remind readers that as early as 1929 Aichele pointed to the early mistakes common to all <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term> manuscripts; his article: <note>German title: "Grundsätzliches zur Kawi-Interpretation"</note> *“Fundamental Principles for the Interpretation of Kawi”*, which appeared in the Dutch publication, FBG I, p. 1—21, has been used by both Dr. Juynboll and Dr. Poerbatjaraka and has not been disputed; (nor, I am afraid to say, paid sufficient attention to). I think his argumentation is perfectly well-founded.

As a consequence I hope that in the next edition of the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</term>, <term>cantos</term> V—VI will be slightly rearranged and will look as follows:

*   **V. 1—89** Rajanī. 
*   **90—107** Vańśastha. 
*   **108—115** Śārdūlavikrīḍitā. 
*   **116** Puṣpitāgrā. 
*   **VI. 1—36** Mālinī; 
*   **37—86** Anuṣṭubh (Vaktra, Śloka); 
*   **87—102** Turagagati; 
*   **103—32** Vasantatilakā; 
*   **133—44** Śikhariṇī; 
*   **145—68** Upajāti; 
*   **169—70** Indravajra; 
*   **171** Upendravajra; 
*   **172—3** Citralekhā; 
*   **174** Dodhaka; 
*   **175** Tanumadhyā; 
*   **176** Mattamayūra 

(There is a slight divergence between the names given by Kern in 1900 and by Poerbatjaraka in 1932; Poerbatjaraka here corrected Kern).

This splitting up of a <term>canto<gloss>sarga; a major division or chapter in a poem</gloss></term> which was not too long (Canto XIV has only 70 stanzas, and XIII has 97, whereas XXI has 248, and XXIV even 260) has been done without apparent reason and without proper care; it is a moot point whether this was done by the original author or by a later editor or interpolator. |---|---|---|---|
| IV. | 3 | verses of | <term>Puṣpitâgrā<gloss>a Sanskrit meter whose name means "with flowering tips," characterized by a varied rhythm at the end of the line</gloss></term> (a meter common to all our <term>courtly epics<gloss>kāvyas</gloss></term>). |
| XVI. | 7 | ,, ,, | <term>Prabhā<gloss>a specific rhythmic meter</gloss></term> (nowhere in our courtly epics used as a concluding stanza). |
| XVII. | 5 | ,, ,, | <term>Mālinī<gloss>a meter of fifteen syllables, often used for elegant descriptions</gloss></term> (common to all our courtly epics). |
| XVIII. | 8 | ,, ,, | <term>Śikhariṇī<gloss>a meter of seventeen syllables, often used for intense emotion</gloss></term> (used in the *Kirātârjunīya* <note>The Hunter and Arjuna</note> and the *Śiśupāla-vadha* <note>The Slaying of Shishupala</note>). |
| XXV. | 9 | ,, ,, | <term>Aparavaktra<gloss>a meter similar to Puṣpitâgrā</gloss></term> (only used in the *Kāmandakīya Nītiśāstra* <note>Kamandaki’s Elements of Polity</note>, but not in our six main courtly epics). |

For it appears from more than 110 canto-endings in our courtly epics, that only 1 or 2 concluding stanzas of a canto are written in a different meter. The exceptions, however, do occasionally appear:

| | | | | | | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bhāravi's | *Kirātârjunīya* | VIII | ends with | 3 | stanzas in | <term>Vasanta-tilakā<gloss>a meter of fourteen syllables, often used for descriptions of nature</gloss></term>; |
| Kālidāsa's | *Raghuvaṃśa* | IX | ,, ,, | 7 | ,, in | <term>Vasanta-tilakā</term>; |
| Śrī Harṣa's | *Naiṣadhīya* | XV | ,, ,, | 10 | ,, in | <term>Śārdūla-vikrīḍitā<gloss>a complex meter of nineteen syllables</gloss></term>. |

These exceptions, however, are far from numerous in our courtly epics, nor do they occur where we need them most: in the *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya*, even though we may assume that <term>Yogîśwara<gloss>the legendary author of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</gloss></term> had not only studied the *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya*. It seems appropriate, in this connection, to point to another peculiarity: the *amplification of the concluding stanzas*.

The first step in this process seems to be: two concluding stanzas of a different meter. Canto VI of the *Raghuvaṃśa* <note>The Dynasty of Raghu</note> runs as follows: stanzas 1–84 are in <term>Indravajra<gloss>a meter of eleven syllables</gloss></term>, 85 is in <term>Mālinī</term>, and 86 is in <term>Puṣpitâgrā</term>; see also Cantos XI and XIX; as well as the *Kumāra-sambhava* <note>The Birth of the War-God</note> Cantos III, IV, XIV, XVI, and XVII; the *Kirātârjunīya* I, IV, VI, XI, and XIV; the *Śiśupāla-vadha* I, VII, XV, and XX; and the *Naiṣadhīya* <note>The Life of Nala</note> VI, X, XI, and XVIII.

The second step is: not 1 *or* 2 but 1 + 2 (or 2 + 1) concluding stanzas: *Raghuvaṃśa* V and XVI; *Kirātârjunīya* IX; *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya* XXI; *Naiṣadhīya* XIII and XVII. Or alternatively, 3 concluding stanzas all in the same (but different from the main body) meter: *Kirātârjunīya* VIII.

The third step seems to be: 3 stanzas of different meters: *Raghuvaṃśa* XII; *Kirātârjunīya* II; *Śiśupāla-vadha* II; and *Naiṣadhīya* I.

<summary>This page analyzes the unusual structure of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa's chapter endings, noting that it contains an unusually high number of concluding verses compared to its Sanskrit predecessors. The author categorizes the increasing complexity of these endings across various classical Indian epics to contextualize the Javanese author's poetic choices.</summary>
<keywords>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, Bhaṭṭi-kāvya, Sanskrit poetics, kāvya, poetic meters, Yogîśwara, sarga endings, comparative literature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical comparison between the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa (OJR) and its Sanskrit precursors, focusing on the number of concluding stanzas in a chapter (sarga) and whether these stanzas must change their poetic meter.</meta>

The fourth step could be: 4 stanzas of **2** different <term>metres<gloss>rhythmic patterns in verse</gloss></term> or of **3**: <term>Kirātārjunīya<gloss>a 6th-century Sanskrit epic about the hero Arjuna and the god Shiva</gloss></term> III, or of 4: <term>Kumārasambhava<gloss>"The Birth of the War-God," an epic by the poet Kālidāsa</gloss></term> XII.

Continuing in this way, we find in our <term>kāvyas<gloss>highly stylized courtly epic poems</gloss></term> the following number of conclusive verses:

5: <term>Raghuvaṁśa<gloss>"The Dynasty of Raghu," one of the most famous Sanskrit epics</gloss></term> VIII; Kirātārjunīya III, IX, XVII; <term>Śiśupālavadha<gloss>"The Slaying of Śiśupāla," a complex epic by the poet Māgha</gloss></term>
6: Śiśupālavadha XVI. <margin>[IX, XX.</margin> <note>These numbers likely refer to the specific cantos in the Śiśupālavadha that contain six concluding stanzas.</note>
7: Kirātārjunīya XV.
9: (<term>Bhaṭṭikāvya<gloss>the specific Sanskrit source text used by the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa poet</gloss></term> II).
10: Kirātārjunīya XVI.
11: (Bhaṭṭikāvya XI).
12: (Bhaṭṭikāvya XXII); <term>Naiṣadhīya<gloss>also known as the Naiṣadha-charita, a late masterpiece of Sanskrit poetry</gloss></term> XIX.
13: Śiśupālavadha VI.

In fact, in several cases the number seems to be higher still, up to some 20; or, in other words, a certain amount of freedom which we find already as early as Kālidāsa’s poems, in later times gave rise to complete freedom and the complete merging of the rules of <term>sarga<gloss>a chapter or canto in an epic</gloss></term> and concluding stanzas. Śrī Harṣa’s *Naiṣadhīya* may end every *sarga* with one stanza of <term>śārdūla-vikrīḍita<gloss>literally "tiger’s play," a long and majestic 19-syllable meter</gloss></term>, but what is one to think of: 1—142 <term>Vaṁśastha<gloss>a 12-syllable meter</gloss></term>, 143 <term>Dodhaka<gloss>an 11-syllable meter</gloss></term>, 144 <term>Vasantatilakā<gloss>a 14-syllable meter</gloss></term>, 145 Śārdūla-vikrīḍita?

Amongst Indian theorists, <term>Daṇḍin<gloss>a renowned 7th-century literary critic and author</gloss></term> in his <term>Kāvyādarśa<gloss>"The Mirror of Poetry," a fundamental manual on Sanskrit poetics</gloss></term> I, 14—9 is rather vague on this point. Referring to the *sargas* of a <term>mahā-kāvya<gloss>a "great epic" or "courtly epic" following specific structural rules</gloss></term>, he prescribes that they should be <note>original Sanskrit: "sarvatra bhinna-vṛttānta"; meaning: "always characterized by varied meter at the end."</note> (Böhtlingk: <note>original German: ". . . Kapitel, in denen . . . der Schluss im Versmaass stets wechselt"; translated: ". . . Chapters in which . . . the meter of the conclusion always changes."</note>).

As a consequence, we may draw this conclusion from this section 7 d: the absence of different final verses could be explained by comparison with the *Bhaṭṭikāvya*, and their apparent redundance in itself presents no difficulty either in the theory of such an authority as Daṇḍin or in the practice of our well-known *kāvyas*. (It is out of the scope of the present investigations to examine whether the final stanzas in some *sargas* of the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>abbreviated as OJR</gloss></term> are rightly suspected by <term>Poerbatjaraka<gloss>a pioneering Javanese scholar of Old Javanese literature (1884–1964)</gloss></term>).

As the number of conclusive verses gives rise to some misunderstanding, it is advisable to have also a look at their kind of metre:

<summary>This page argues that the "excess" of concluding stanzas in the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa is not a mistake, as similar variation is found in major Sanskrit epics and supported by classical Indian literary theory. It lists various examples from famous Sanskrit poets like Kālidāsa and Māgha to show that the number and style of ending stanzas could be quite flexible.</summary>

<summary>This page presents a comparative table showing the frequency and variety of poetic meters used in the concluding stanzas of chapters across the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa and five major Sanskrit epics. It demonstrates that the Old Javanese text employs a significantly higher variety of meters (19 types) compared to its Indian counterparts.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis comparing the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa (OJR) with Sanskrit court epics. While the previous page provided a statistical table of poetic meters used in chapter conclusions, this page interprets those findings and introduces the theory that the OJR was intended as a technical manual for metrics.</meta>

Sri Harsha’s *The Story of Nala* <note>original: "Naiṣadhīya"</note> has been left out of the picture, because its poet was satisfied to use only the <term>Tiger's Play meter<gloss>śārdūlavikrīḍita</gloss></term> as a concluding stanza to each and every <term>chapter<gloss>sarga</gloss></term>.

We could draw the following conclusions from this chart: there is considerable freedom for variety of choice of metre for the concluding stanza(s).

The <term>Garland-Maker meter<gloss>Mālinī</gloss></term> (36 ✕) enjoys pre-eminence; next comes the <term>Tiger's Play meter<gloss>Śārdūlavikrīḍita</gloss></term> (5 + 22 ✕), the <term>Flowery-Tips meter<gloss>Puṣpitāgrā</gloss></term> (10 ✕), the <term>Slowly-Stepping meter<gloss>Mandarakrāntā</gloss></term> (12 ✕), the <term>Ornament of Spring meter<gloss>Vasantalika</gloss></term> (11 ✕), the <term>Gladdener meter<gloss>Praharṣiṇī</gloss></term> (9 ✕), the <term>Tiger's Play meter<gloss>Sārdūlavikrīḍita</gloss></term> <note>Spelled with a "S" here in the original OCR; likely a variation of the same meter.</note> (5 ✕) & the <term>Doe meter<gloss>Hariṇī</gloss></term> (5 ✕).

Yogīśwara in the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>OJR</gloss></term> uses most of the much-loved concluding metres, several of which are less frequently applied by the poets of our <term>courtly epics<gloss>kāvyas</gloss></term>, and nine of which are not used as such in our other <term>courtly epics<gloss>kāvyas</gloss></term>.

In choosing 19 different conclusive metres, Yogīśwara definitely strove after more than the usual variety, as will appear still more strikingly in his choice of metres for the narrative parts.

e) The figures given in section 5e show nothing unusual in columns 2 & 4, but the variation of metres (3) is considerably more frequent than in any of our <term>courtly epics<gloss>kāvyas</gloss></term>; the number of different metres (1) is double that of Māgha’s <term>epic<gloss>kāvya</gloss></term> <note>Referring to the Śiśupālavadha</note>, and 4½ times as much as the average of the other <term>courtly epics<gloss>kāvyas</gloss></term>. No wonder that 7 of the 81 metres used have not yet been met with even in such a collection as the *Jayadāman* <note>A famous collection of Sanskrit texts on prosody.</note>.

The <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>OJR</gloss></term> not only uses a large variety of metres and changes them rather frequently, but it avoids using a single one of them so often that in the long run it would appear to have been preferred (see page 29).

These 17 metres are the most frequently used ones, in the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>OJR</gloss></term> as well as in our <term>courtly epics<gloss>kāvyas</gloss></term>; the other 64 different metres together are divided over 40% of the verses. The greatest amount of stanzas in one metre ever used in the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>OJR</gloss></term> is the <term>Cane-Like meter<gloss>Vaṃśastha</gloss></term>, at less than 8%. The difference between our <term>courtly epics<gloss>kāvyas</gloss></term> is not only considerable, but the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>OJR</gloss></term> clearly in this respect is conceived differently. In the beginning we still find 60 of the <term>Noble meter<gloss>Āryā</gloss></term> (Chapter I), 77 of the <term>Ornament of Spring meter<gloss>Vasantatilaka</gloss></term> (Chapter II), 89 of the <term>Night meter<gloss>Rajanī</gloss></term> (Chapter V) & 79 of the <term>Recurrent-Joy meter<gloss>Punarmada</gloss></term> (Chapter VIII) — but that is the end, and beginning from here Yogīśwara went his own way in the choice and frequency of metres; how he used all of them can be seen in detail in the table of contents.

Musing over this state of affairs, and the fact that the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa's<gloss>OJR</gloss></term> example is a textbook <note>Referring to the Bhaṭṭikāvya, which was designed to teach Sanskrit grammar.</note>, one feels inclined to surmise that Yogīśwara in his turn has deliberately also written a textbook—not on grammar and <term>poetic ornamentation<gloss>alaṅkāra</gloss></term> like his example, but on metrics. If that were true, he would have surpassed his example by far in accomplishing his task in such an

<summary>This page analyzes the unprecedented variety of poetic meters in the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, noting that Yogīśwara used 81 different meters. The author suggests the poem was intentionally composed as a textbook for teaching metrics, surpassing its Sanskrit models in technical complexity.</summary>
<keywords>Yogīśwara, Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, Sanskrit prosody, poetic meters, comparative literature, textbook theory, Śārdūlavikrīḍita, Vaṃśastha</keywords>

...in an unobtrusive way; much less obtrusive also than the later *Collection of Meters* <gloss>Wṛtta-sañcaya</gloss> written by the author <term>Tan-akung</term> <note>A famous Javanese poet from the late Majapahit period known for his expertise in metrics.</note>.

f) True to the customs found in <term>courtly epics<gloss>kāvyas</gloss></term>, the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>OJR</gloss></term> in <term>chapter<gloss>sarga</gloss></term> XXV shows 37 changes of metre, which is the average for such works.

g) A manuscript page of a Javanese poetical text is as densely packed with letters as a South-Indian temple is crowded with images and carvings; no square inch has been left unused. Repeatedly the metre changes, and since neither the script nor this style of writing and printing allows the reader to get a sense of the structure at a glance, a clever strategy has been evolved. In the last line before a new meter—for example, the <term>Youthful meter<gloss>sinom</gloss></term>—begins, the word "young" <gloss>anom</gloss> or "youthful" <gloss>sinoman</gloss> is used as a hint or introduction; in the last line before the new meter <term>Withdrawal<gloss>paṅkur</gloss></term> begins, the word "to turn back" <gloss>muṅkur</gloss> or "behind" <gloss>puṅkur</gloss> is used, and so on. This tells the reader exactly how the next section should be sung or chanted.

The <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>OJR</gloss></term> also inserts the name of its meter into the text, but, curiously enough, either...

<summary>This page provides a statistical table comparing the usage frequency of various poetic meters in the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa against major Sanskrit epics, highlighting the diversity of the Javanese work. It also explains the "stratagem" used by Javanese poets to signal changes in meter to performers through clever wordplay within the verses.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa (OJR), transitioning from a statistical comparison of poetic meters to the specific literary devices used to identify those meters within the text. It then begins a new section (§ 8) analyzing the narrative content and its deviations from the Sanskrit prototype, the Bhaṭṭi-kāvya (BhK).</meta>

at the end of the 4th line of one of the first stanzas of a new metre, or even in the last line of the whole group of stanzas where this metre has been used. So VII. 54 d has as its last word *śārdūla-vikrīḍitā* <gloss>tiger’s play meter</gloss>, the name of the metre used in stanzas 43—54; and 56 d ends with the word *Tvaritagati* <gloss>fast-paced meter</gloss>, the metre used in the stanzas 55—70; 71 ends with *Tanumadhyā* <gloss>slender-waisted meter</gloss>, the metre used in this one stanza of its kind. The method followed in Modern Javanese poetry seems definitely an improvement on the old one.

It is curious that only in 45 cases out of 282, <term>OJR<gloss>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</gloss></term> makes its metre known by this device, mainly in the <term>*sargas*<gloss>cantos or chapters</gloss></term> XVI—XXI. In the table of contents the names of metres inserted in the poem are put into italics. A system or reason could not be discovered; but roughly the frequent denoting of metres coincides with the battle scenes, only to end with the appearance of Prahasta, Rāwaṇa’s <note>The primary antagonist and demon king of Lankā</note> right-hand man. This, however, is only an observation, not an explanation.

### § 8. *OJR. Contents.*

The peculiarities which distinguish <term>BhK<gloss>Bhaṭṭi-kāvya, a 7th-century Sanskrit poem</gloss></term> + OJR from Vālmīki <note>The legendary sage credited with composing the original Sanskrit Rāmāyaṇa</note> have been dealt with in § 6. 
A detailed table of contents has been added as Appendix I; here one sees printed in italics nearly all stanzas which are not contained in BhK and which thus are additions in OJR. It is remarkable that from these numerous passages, several of them decidedly longish, some of them even repetitions, and one even offering a contradiction, only four stanzas, VI, 106—9, have been recognised as additions or interpolations by the four scholars who have studied the OJR. These passages deserve to be examined together.

It seemed advisable to split up the material chiefly into two main groups. For in reading the last third of OJR it becomes manifestly clear that Yogîśwara <note>The traditionally attributed author or lead editor of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</note> had freed himself completely from his prototype BhK and must have known another recension <note>A specific version or revision of a text</note> or other recensions. Which these are, even Dr. Bulcke <note>Camille Bulcke, a famous scholar of the Rāmāyaṇa</note> could not find out. Detailed comparison of BhK/OJR shows us, that from the very beginning Yogîśwara was not dependent upon BhK only, but was in a position to narrate properly a situation which Bhaṭṭi considered as known to his students (names of Daśaratha’s <note>The King of Ayodhya and father of Rama</note> queens e.g.), or which were inappropriate for him (list of names of monkey & <term>rākṣasa<gloss>demon or ogre</gloss></term> officers). 

Though fully realising that some arbitrariness cannot be avoided, I distinguish mainly these 2 groups: II better narration, addition of detail,

Finally, it is worth investigating which passages found in the <term>BhK<gloss>Bhaṭṭi-kāvya, the Sanskrit source text</gloss></term> were not included in the <term>OJR<gloss>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</gloss></term>, or were shortened.
