This large addition of 201 stanzas begins with the episode of the illusory heads of Rāma and <term>Lakṣmaṇa<gloss>Rāma's brother</gloss></term>, to which the <term>Bhaṭṭi-kāvya<gloss>The Sanskrit poem that served as a primary source for the Javanese version</gloss></term> (XIV. 1) only alludes. <note>original: "tato Daśâsyaḥ smara-vihvalâtmā, cāra-prakāśī-kṛta-śatru-śaktiḥ, vimohya māyā-maya-Rāma-mūrdhnā, Sītām anīkaṁ prajighāya yoddhum."</note> This passage describes how the love-troubled <term>Rāwaṇa<gloss>The demon king, also called 'Ten-Faces'</gloss></term>, having learned of the enemy's strength through his spies, deceived Sītā with a magically created head of Rāma and then sent his army out to fight. (This is followed by the episode found in Prof. Brough’s “Selections from Classical Sanskrit Literature” p. 104). In this connection, Dr. Bulcke points to the <term>Abhiṣeka-Nātaka<gloss>The Coronation Play</gloss></term> and the <term>Mahā-Nātaka<gloss>The Great Play</gloss></term>.

In his enlightening paper, “*The Three Recensions of the Vālmīki Rāmāyaṇa*”, (Journal of Oriental Research XVII, 1 Sept. 1947, 1949, p. 1—32), Dr. Bulcke offers a useful “Comparative Table.” In this table, he uses the most widely spread version—labeled the *Southern* recension for lack of a better name—as the standard of comparison. He then provides notes for each <term>Kāṇḍa<gloss>A major book or section of the epic</gloss></term> separately:

**A.** Subject matter found in the Southern version but missing from one or both of the other versions;
**B.** Subject matter missing from the Southern version but present in one or both of the others;
**C.** Other differences that cannot be categorized under A or B, or which are of minor importance.

As a means of identifying the origins of large portions of <term>Yogīśwara’s<gloss>The traditional name for the poet of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</gloss></term> poem, this paper has proven to be very promising. However, while this subject is important in itself, it lies beyond our current focus.

Perhaps I may offer my preliminary impression, which is subject to revision and therefore not included in the "Conclusions" in the next paragraph.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa (OJR) and its relationship to its Sanskrit sources, specifically the Bhaṭṭi-kāvya (BhK). The previous page detailed specific narrative additions in the Javanese version, such as Sītā’s letter and the construction of the bridge to Laṅkā, which diverge from the Sanskrit original.</meta>

...that Yogîśwara did not use a second source, but worked on his poem under the motto: “I take my goods where I find them” <note>original: "Je prends mon bien où je le trouve"</note>. Perhaps it will be possible one day to make acceptable that he mastered his considerable knowledge of Sanskrit in India itself, where he learned many versions of the Rāmāyaṇa—a support to the scholar Bosch’s thesis concerning Indonesian students going to India, just as we know the Chinese did.

## IV. CONCLUSIONS

a) *Concerning the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa (OJR).*

1. As Dr. Poerbatjaraka had concluded, Yogîśwara knew Sanskrit very well.
2. He created the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, initially in close connection with the <term>Bhaṭṭi-kāvya<gloss>A 7th-century Sanskrit poem that serves as the primary model for the Javanese version</gloss></term> (BhK), (according to the scholars Ghosh and Bulcke), but then, for reasons not yet clear to us, he abandoned his model in the last third of his work.
3. To conclude his poem he seems to have borrowed from the "flotsam and jetsam" of general knowledge concerning the Rāmāyaṇa that was current in those days in South East Asia; at least Dr. Bulcke could not discover a particular specific source.
4. He might have felt attracted to the Bhaṭṭi-kāvya because of its outspoken <term>Śivaitic<gloss>Related to the worship of the god Shiva</gloss></term> character, since this appears also manifestly in the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa (as noted by Poerbatjaraka).
5. He may also have had a certain preference for the Bhaṭṭi-kāvya as a poem or it may have haunted him as a textbook.
6. The examples of Sanskrit grammar, however, in <term>sargas<gloss>chapters or cantos of a Sanskrit poem</gloss></term> I—IV, V—IX, and XIV to the end were of no use to him for the composition of a poem in the Old Javanese language.
7. The tenth chapter of the Bhaṭṭi-kāvya is on <term>alaṅkāra<gloss>poetic ornaments or figures of speech</gloss></term>, the eleventh on <term>mādhurya<gloss>the aesthetic quality of sweetness</gloss></term>, and the twelfth on <term>bhāvika<gloss>the quality of vivid, emotional description</gloss></term>; these three topics are treated at some length in <term>Daṇḍin’s Kāvyādarśa<gloss>A foundational Sanskrit manual on poetics and literary theory</gloss></term>. Yogîśwara here still followed rather closely his example (though not so closely as in the first chapters, perhaps); then he inserted his own Cantos XVII—XIX.11 and for the rest either kept his former example at a distance or even did completely without it. The reason? Only guesses can be made. He may himself as a student never have mastered the whole Bhaṭṭi-kāvya. He may have worked with a manuscript which was defective, became defective, or got lost. Perhaps Yogîśwara, working in a language without any connection with the grammatical niceties dealt with in the final chapters of the Sanskrit original, simply lost interest.

<summary>This page concludes that the author Yogîśwara was a master of Sanskrit who likely studied in India, using the Bhaṭṭi-kāvya as a model before diverging into local traditions and his own poetic style. The author suggests the Javanese poet eventually abandoned the Sanskrit model because its complex grammatical lessons were irrelevant to the Old Javanese language.</summary>

<keywords>Yogîśwara, Bhaṭṭi-kāvya, Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, Sanskrit poetics, India-Indonesia relations, Poerbatjaraka, literary sources, sargas, alaṅkāra, Daṇḍin</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the conclusion of Section IV regarding the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa (OJR). It moves from discussing the author Yogīśwara’s technical skill to the broader classification of this work within the tradition of Old Javanese court poetry (kakawins).</meta>

8. As early as 1926, the scholar Aichele already pointed to Yogīśwara’s use of <term>Anuprāsa<gloss>alliteration or the repetition of similar sounds</gloss></term>, <term>Yamakas<gloss>wordplay or "chime" rhymes where words with different meanings sound alike</gloss></term>, <term>Upamās<gloss>similes or direct comparisons</gloss></term>, <term>Rūpaka<gloss>metaphors</gloss></term>, <term>Utprekṣā<gloss>poetic exaggeration or vivid illustrations</gloss></term>, and <term>Apahnuti<gloss>a rhetorical figure where the true nature of something is denied to suggest a hidden quality</gloss></term> (also called *Nihnuti*); in the preceding sections, we have seen where Yogīśwara may have learned them. He may have learned even more; this remains to be investigated, as this treatise must have its limits somewhere.

9. The difficult and artificial passages, which continued to arouse the suspicion of having been <term>interpolated<gloss>inserted by a later author rather than being part of the original text</gloss></term>, even after the appearance of Aichele’s pioneering articles, should for the moment be considered part and parcel of Yogīśwara’s <term>magnum opus<gloss>greatest work</gloss></term> (and consequently, they should be added to the translations).

10. Not only do the erotic passages of the <term>Bhaṭṭi-kāvya<gloss>The Sanskrit model for the poem</gloss></term> (Book XI) and the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa (Book XII) prove to be common to both poems—proving they are genuine and not later additions or even elaborations—but furthermore, we must generally be prepared to accept Indian standards in these fields (points 8, 9, and 10) as laid down in the *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya* and Bhaṭṭi’s textbooks. (These textbooks, as experts in Indian studies will know, have not yet been identified; perhaps they are lost, or perhaps they have not yet been recognized among the numerous Sanskrit manuscripts whose descriptions have not yet been published). It remains to Aichele’s credit to have proposed all of this, and to Prijono’s credit to have repeated it.

### b) *The traditional distinction: The Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa versus the other Kakawins.*

The situation at the moment is this: arguments have been brought forward to prove a number of things concerning the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa. This entire line of reasoning gains considerably in importance as soon as it is accepted that it also applies to the other 50 or perhaps even 100 <term>kakawins<gloss>Old Javanese poems written in Indian-inspired meters, typically composed at court</gloss></term>. Are they similar? Do they indeed form one group? It has never been denied, even though their dates of composition seem to be separated by two centuries. A scholar like Professor Dr. R. M. Ng. Poerbatjaraka—who edited and translated *The Marriage of Arjuna*, *The Burning of the God of Love*, and *The Tale of the Worthless*, and who translated *The War of the Bharatas*, *The Realm Ordered by Sacred Tradition*, and the *Rāmāyaṇa*, and who made extracts from *The Career of Krishna*, *The Marriage of Bhīma*, *Death by the Sumanasa Flower*, and *The Tale of Sutasoma* <note>99</note>—always held this point of view. Aichele also took this for granted and brought forward new evidence. Scholars such as Berg, Sarkar, Prijono, and Teeuw (translator of the *Poem of Bhoma*, and editor and translator of *Hari-

---

99) <note>original: "Indonesische Handschriften door R. M. Ng. Dr. Poerbatjaraka, Dr. P. Voorhoeve en Dr. C. Hooykaas, Lembaga Kebudajaan Indonesia 'Koninklijk Bataviaasch Genootschap van Kunsten en Wetenschappen', 1950. Woord vooraf."</note> *Indonesian Manuscripts* by R. M. Ng. Dr. Poerbatjaraka, Dr. P. Voorhoeve, and Dr. C. Hooykaas, Indonesian Cultural Institute "Royal Batavian Society of Arts and Sciences," 1950. Foreword.

<summary>This page argues that the complex poetic devices and "artificial" passages in the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa are original features modeled on Indian standards rather than later additions. It also introduces the question of whether these findings apply to the entire genre of kakawin (Old Javanese court poetry).</summary>

<keywords>Yogīśwara, Sanskrit poetics, Bhaṭṭikāvya, Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, kakawin tradition, Poerbatjaraka, literary interpolation, Indian influence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the structural classification of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa (OJR). The author is addressing the scholarly consensus established by earlier researchers like Kern, Gunning, and Stutterheim regarding the poem's lineage and its relationship to other courtly poems.</meta>

...lineage) maintain the same view, not to forget the work of scholars like Kern, Gunning, and Stutterheim <note>See footnote 100 below.</note>.
There remains, however, one objection, one small impish "but":

| Poem Title | Structure | | Count | Meter Style | Division Unit | Comparison |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa | consists | of | 26 | <term>poly-metric<gloss>using multiple poetic meters</gloss></term> | <term>sargas<gloss>large chapters or books, following Indian epic style</gloss></term>, | whereas: |
| *Bhoma-kāwya* <note>The Poem of Bhoma</note> | ,, | ,, | 188 | <term>mono-metric<gloss>using only one poetic meter per section</gloss></term> | <term>cantos<gloss>shorter musical or poetic divisions typical of Javanese poetry</gloss></term> | |

| *Smara-dahana* <note>The Burning of the Love God</note> | ,, | ,, | 40 | ,, | ,, | |

| *Bhārata-yuddha* <note>The Great War of the Bharatas</note> | ,, | ,, | 52 | ,, | ,, | |
| *Nāgarakṛtāgama* <note>The Realm Ordered by Sacred Tradition</note> | ,, | ,, | 98 | ,, | ,, | |

We are in the habit of taking the last-mentioned six poems (plus the other <term>kakawins<gloss>Old Javanese courtly poems</gloss></term>) together, and separating the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa from them only because of the *sarga/canto* question <note>This refers to whether the poem is divided into large, varied Indian-style chapters or shorter, uniform Javanese-style sections.</note>. The aim of this section, IV. b, is to prove that this distinction is not actually relevant, and that the first six poems listed are closer to each other in spirit than they are to the style found in the *Nāgarakṛtāgama*.

Comparison with the *Bhoma-kāwya* again makes the difficulties seem not insuperable, to say the very least. For it becomes apparent that either the author Yogīśwara, a later <term>redactor<gloss>editor who revises a text</gloss></term>, or even a humble copyist split up *Bhoma-kāwya* Book IX into Rāmāyaṇa IX and X, and split XII into XIII and XIV, and XIII into XV and XVI. In doing so, they omitted to add new concluding verses to the new Book XIII and the new ending of Book V. One gets the impression that the Indian theory concerning the division into *sargas* and the necessity of "concluding verses" <note>Verses at the end of a chapter usually written in a different meter to signal a transition.</note> had gradually been forgotten and lost. 

Moreover, the freedom with which the Indian poets handled the subject of concluding verses was completely baffling for those who had to deduce the principle from the actual practice of our <term>kāvyas<gloss>highly stylized Indian court poems</gloss></term> or the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa. In the Indian *kāvyas*, the practice of adding concluding stanzas had extended to a dozen, or even a score, or even a complete *sarga*! And in the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, one stanza of a new meter was repeatedly introduced somewhere in the middle of a *sarga*. (We do not find this in the two poems by the great Indian poet Kālidāsa, but later poems among our other *kāvyas* have many such isolated stanzas).

In section 7, attention has been drawn to the fact that Yogīśwara, while mainly following his model in the division into *sargas*, again broke them up into a much greater number of smaller entities. These are exactly comparable to the so-called "cantos" of the *kakawins*.

---

100) <note>original: "Cultuurgeschiedenis van Indonesië II. Het Hindoeïsme in de Archipel"</note> Cultural History of Indonesia II: Hinduism in the Archipelago 2, 1951, Wolters, Groningen/Jakarta, p. 150-4.

<summary>This page argues that the structural differences between the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa and other kakawins are less significant than previously thought, suggesting that the poem's division into chapters was likely altered by later editors who lost touch with Indian poetic rules.</summary>
<keywords>sarga vs canto, Yogīśwara, kakawin structure, Indian poetic theory, Bhoma-kāwya, poly-metric vs mono-metric</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the structural evolution of Old Javanese poetry, specifically comparing the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa (OJR) to its Indian predecessor, the Bhaṭṭi-kāvya (BhK), and later Javanese works.</meta>

Appendix III shows this much quicker and better than words can do.

The longer a <term>kāvya<gloss>a sophisticated, courtly epic poem written in a dense, ornate style</gloss></term> is, the longer are the mono-metric <note>Sections written using only a single poetic meter.</note> passages it contains, and generally also the more of them there are (this, of course, can also be postulated the other way round). *Kumāra-sambhava* <note>The Birth of the War God (by Kalidasa)</note> has only fairly long mono-metric passages and conclusive stanzas; *Raghuvaṁśa* <note>The Dynasty of Raghu (by Kalidasa)</note> has a few small mono-metric passages; *Kirātārjunīya* <note>Arjuna and the Mountain Man</note>, *Śiśupālavadha* <note>The Slaying of Shishupala</note> and *Naiṣadhīya* <note>The Story of the King of Nishadha</note> also only a few. But here the practice of the "show-metres" or "show-sargas" <note>Virtuosic displays where a poet demonstrates their technical skill by using difficult or rare meters.</note> makes its entry, and *Naiṣadhīya*, long as it is, on the one hand, and containing the greatest number of mono-metric passages and also the longest of them, on the other hand introduces quite a number of very short metrical passages. *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya* <note>The Poem of Bhatti, the direct Indian model for the Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</note>, the first courtly poem to have mono-metric passages of every length, still fits in very well with our other courtly poems.

The Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa maintains in great lines its model's division into <term>sargas<gloss>large chapters or books, following Indian epic style</gloss></term> and also still contains not less than 11 passages of 40–90 mono-metric stanzas, but it splits up the *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya's* 6 longer passages into a considerable number of smaller ones. It cannot be said that the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa introduced a completely new procedure, but the whole effect upon the listener to this complete poem must have been considerably different. This becomes still more evident when we remember that: *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya* used 23 different metres, and did 74% in <term>Vaktra<gloss>a common 8-syllable meter, also known as Anuṣṭubh</gloss></term>, while its "imitation," the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, used 81 different metres and did less than 8% at the most in any single metre.

This only tends to prove that the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, closely related as it is to the *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya* regarding contents, went in an opposite direction in the field of variety of metres, largely developing possibilities which some of our Indian courtly poems had underdeveloped, specially the didactic ones. Seen superficially, the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa with its 26 chapters completely fits in with our Indian courtly poems; in fact, however, a process of disintegration of the larger mono-metric passages had already gone rather far.

The Old Javanese *Rāmāyaṇa* has some 268 metrical changes in 2,774 stanzas; the *Bhoma-kāvya* <note>The Poem of Bhoma</note> has some 118 cantos, which equals 117 metrical changes in 1,375 stanzas. The average is more or less the same. The picture of the mono-metric passages offers a striking resemblance. But *Bhoma-kāvya* shows no longer a trace of the chapter-structure; this was already <term>in decline<gloss>original: "en décadence"</gloss></term> in the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, and seems never to have taken solid root on Javanese soil. There are still 7 one-stanza-cantos, 10 two-stanza-cantos—queer cantos indeed, apparently the remainder of a former conclusive or intermediary stanza or stanzas. These separate stanzas soon became obsolete; *Hari-waṅśa* <note>The Lineage of Vishnu</note>, *Smara-dahana* <note>The Burning of the Love God</note> and *Arjuna-wiwāha* <note>The Marriage of Arjuna</note> still have a few of them, but that passes.

<summary>The author argues that while the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa appears to follow Indian structural models on the surface, it actually pioneered a shift toward extreme metrical variety and the eventual abandonment of the Indian 'sarga' (chapter) system in favor of shorter Javanese 'cantos'.</summary>
<keywords>metrical variety, sarga, canto, Bhaṭṭi-kāvya, Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, Bhoma-kāvya, Anuṣṭubh, poetic structure, evolution of Javanese literature</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the structural comparison of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa to other courtly poems, moving from the specific comparison with its Indian model to its place within the wider tradition of Javanese literature.</meta>

The great formal difference apparently lies not so much between the <term>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa<gloss>The oldest major work of Old Javanese literature, retelling the story of Rama; often abbreviated as OJR.</gloss></term> (divided into poly-metric <term>sargas<gloss>Large poetic chapters composed of many different meters, following Indian epic style.</gloss></term>) and the other <term>Kakawins<gloss>Traditional courtly epic poems written in Old Javanese using complex rhythmic patterns derived from Sanskrit.</gloss></term> (divided into mono-metric cantos <note>Poetic sections that use only a single rhythmic pattern throughout.</note>), since that whole borrowed *sarga*-superstruc-
ture, and the feeling for long mono-metric passages proved to be uncongenial to <note>The traditional name attributed to the author or primary editor of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa.</note> Yogîśwara. It lies more between the first 6 *Kakawins* and the <note>A famous 14th-century Javanese court poem, also known as the Desawarnana, which describes the Majapahit kingdom.</note> *Nāgarakṛtâgama*: the difference between meat and minced meat; for that is the appearance of the *Nāgarakṛtâgama’s* short cantos.
Nobody has ever objected to including *Nāgarakṛtâgama* in the category of *Kakawins*; nor do I, provided that, on the other side, the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa be included without (more) reservations.
The Indian theory as expressed in <note>A 7th-century Sanskrit manual on poetic theory titled "The Mirror of Poetry."</note> Daṇḍin’s *Kāvyâdarśa*, referred to earlier, does not weaken this point of view.

### c) *Concerning Old Javanese Kakawin.*

Once we accept the unity of the group of *Kakawins*, the conclusions drawn with reference to the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa must be accepted for them as well. It seems superfluous to repeat them; to be on the safe side, however, I must point to the possibility of interpolations <note>Sections of text added to a manuscript by later scribes or editors rather than the original author.</note>, not because some pas-
sages are long-winded and consist of repetitions, not because they are difficult, artificial or erotical, not because they do not conform to our preconceived standards of literary taste and decency, but because a redactor liked them, or a pedant thought them indispensable. Yogîśwara himself added hundreds of verses in comparison with his example <note>Likely referring to the Sanskrit Bhaṭṭi-kāvya, the primary source for the Javanese version.</note> (§ 8). Why should this process have stopped with him, contrary to so many other *Rāmāyaṇa*-recensions <note>Different versions or editions of the Ramayana story found across various cultures and eras.</note>? Only we should be less prejudiced and more cautious than before, specially in studying the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, and generally in studying Old Javanese *Kakawin*.

<summary>The author argues that the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa belongs to the same genre as later courtly poems (Kakawins) despite structural differences in chapter length and meter. He also warns researchers to be cautious when identifying later additions to these texts, noting that the original authors often expanded upon their source material themselves.</summary>
<keywords>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, Kakawin poetry, poetic structure, Yogishwara, Nagarakrtagama, Dandin, literary theory, interpolations, manuscript tradition</keywords>

| | 65–73 | <term>aupacchandasika<gloss>A lyrical Sanskrit meter often used for elegant or descriptive passages.</gloss></term> | The demoness Śūrpanakhā is described as shameless (53). She reveals her identity and threatens them (56) with the demon brothers Khara and Dūṣaṇa (57), with whom she seeks refuge (58). She gives her version of the events (59-64), invoking their help for revenge (65-6). The demons Triśirah, Khara, and Dūṣaṇa (67) promise to kill Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa (68); they approach the hermitage (69) and take up battle positions (70). Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa kill all the Rākṣasas <note>demons</note> (71); the text provides details of their deaths (72-3). |
| | 74–6 | <term>puṣpitāgrā<gloss>A meter whose name means "with flowery tips," usually signaling a change in tone or a concluding section.</gloss></term> | Triśirah attacks once more (74) and is definitely killed (75). His death is described as frightful; the hermits in the area are thoroughly afraid (76). |
| **5.** | 1–89 | <term>rajani<gloss>A poetic meter; the name literally means "night."</gloss></term> | Khara and Dūṣaṇa approach again (1) but are all conquered (2). Śūrpanakhā flees to the demon king Rāvaṇa (3) and reproaches him (4-5). She reports *how Khara, Dūṣaṇa, and Triśirah were killed by Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa (6)* and *incites Rāvaṇa to take revenge (7)*. She rebukes his thoughtless pursuit of pleasure (8) and tells him how carefree the hermits are *living* (9-10), let alone how Rāma is living (*11*). Again she incites him (12), telling him of Sītā’s beauty (13), loveliness (14), desirability (15), and unsurpassable nature (16). Rāvaṇa reassures her (17-20) and flies to the encounter (21). He meets the demon Mārīca, who warns Rāvaṇa against this course of action (22-3). Mārīca points to the fate of Jāmadagni <note>also known as Paraśu-Rāma or "Rāma with the Axe"</note>, who was defeated by Rāma (24), as well as the death of the demoness Tāṭakā (25), the death of the three Rākṣasa brothers (26), and Rāma’s breaking of the bow at the <term>swayamvara<gloss>A ceremony where a princess chooses a husband from among suitors; in this case, Sītā's marriage contest.</gloss></term> (27), while Rāvaṇa is merely a drunkard (28). Rāvaṇa is angry (29) and scolds Mārīca (30). He makes excuses for Tāṭakā's failure (31), accuses Mārīca of feebleness (32), and pretends that the great bow Rāma broke was actually rotten and eaten by insects (33), and that the other demons were simply careless (34). He scolds Mārīca (35) and threatens him (36). Mārīca excuses himself (37) and promises to help (38), first by eluding Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa by assuming the shape of a golden deer (39). He approaches Sītā (40), who asks Rāma to catch the deer for her (41). Lakṣmaṇa is left behind to take care of Sītā (42). The deer always escapes (43) but is at last shot by Rāma (44); it lets out a frightful cry (45). Hearing the cry, Sītā sends Lakṣmaṇa to help (46). *Lakṣmaṇa is unwilling (47) and does not believe the cry came from Rāma (48-53).* Sītā, out of her mind with worry (54), *accuses and offends Lakṣmaṇa* <note>implying he wishes Rāma dead so he can have her for himself</note> (55-9), *not realizing what she is doing* (60). Lakṣmaṇa ruefully answers (61), *defending his honor* (62), and *prophesying that evil will come to her* (63). Sītā is left picking flowers (64). Rāvaṇa appears, disguised as a wandering monk (65-8), and extolls her beauty (69-78). Sītā is bashful (79) and praises Rāma's virtues (80-4). |
| **6.** | 1–18 | <term>vaṁśastha<gloss>A common 12-syllable meter used for narrative progression.</gloss></term> | Sītā continues to praise Rāma (85-89). Rāvaṇa gradually begins using "me" and "I" to reveal himself (1-4). Rāvaṇa seizes Sītā and abducts her (5). Sītā (6) *laments* her fate (7-15). The vulture king Jaṭāyu hears her, flies in that direction, and challenges Rāvaṇa (16-18). |
| | 19–26 | <term>śārdūlavikrīḍita<gloss>A complex 19-syllable meter; the name means "the play of the tiger," often used for intense or heroic scenes.</gloss></term> | Jaṭāyu attacks Rāvaṇa successfully (19-21). Rāvaṇa’s chariot is damaged and his horses are wounded (22). Jaṭāyu manages to take Sītā (23). Rāvaṇa is ashamed (24). Jaṭāyu, being old, grows slow (25). Rāvaṇa cuts off one of Jaṭāyu’s wings (26). |
| | 27 | <term>puṣpitāgrā<gloss>See previous definition; here used to conclude the episode.</gloss></term> | Rāvaṇa seizes Sītā again and, using a new chariot, abducts her to the island of Laṅkā. |

<summary>This page outlines the pivotal events of the Aranya-kanda, including the defeat of the demon brothers Khara and Dūṣaṇa, the deception of the golden deer (Mārīca), and the abduction of Sītā by Rāvaṇa despite the heroic intervention of the vulture Jaṭāyu.</summary>
<keywords>Śūrpanakhā, Khara, Dūṣaṇa, Triśirah, Rāvaṇa, Mārīca, golden deer, Sītā's abduction, Jaṭāyu, Lakṣmaṇa's defense, Sanskrit meters</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the structural outline of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa. While the previous page focused on the battle with the demons in the forest (the Aranya-kāṇḍa), this section transitions into the search for the abducted Sītā and the formation of the crucial alliance between Rāma and the monkey kingdom (the Kiṣkindha-kāṇḍa).</meta>

28-63 <term>mālinī<gloss>A lyrical Sanskrit meter known as "the garlanded one," often used for descriptive or emotional scenes.</gloss></term>

64-113 <term>anuṣṭubh<gloss>The most standard epic meter, consisting of four lines of eight syllables each.</gloss></term>
(<term>vaktra<gloss>Another name for the anuṣṭubh meter, referring to the "mouth" or opening of the verse.</gloss></term>)

114-29 <term>turagagati<gloss>A meter whose name means "the gait of a horse," suggesting a rhythmic, galloping pace.</gloss></term>

130-59 <term>vasantatilaka<gloss>A rhythmic meter meaning "the ornament of spring."</gloss></term>

160-71 <term>śikhariṇī<gloss>A meter meaning "the peaked one," often used to convey intense emotion or grandeur.</gloss></term>

172-95 <term>upajāti<gloss>A "composite" meter formed by mixing lines of similar structures.</gloss></term>
(mixture of <term>indravajra<gloss>Indra's thunderbolt meter</gloss></term> & <term>upendravajra<gloss>The lesser Indra's thunderbolt meter</gloss></term>)

196-7 <term>indravajra<gloss>A meter named after the thunderbolt weapon of the god Indra.</gloss></term>

<column-break/>

Rāwaṇa continues to woo Sītā (28-30), but Sītā rejects him (31). Rāma is sad (32) and laments (33); he is approached by Lakṣmaṇa (34), who reports the situation (35). Rāma is sad (36), laments (37-47), and faints (48). He bathes (49), performs his religious oblations, and visits the scene of the struggle (50); his impressions of the battlefield (51-53). His indignation (54-55) and challenge (56-59). Lakṣmaṇa approaches Rāma (60) and tries to restrain him (61-63).

Rāma is calmed (64). The vulture king Jaṭāyu is found (65); initially suspected by Rāma (66), he makes himself known (67). Rāma is reconciled (68). Jaṭāyu is very feeble (69), gives his report, and dies (70). *Rāma bewails his father's old friend* (71-73) *and burns his corpse* (74). An ogre (75) named <term>Dīrghabāhu<gloss>Literally "Long-arms," a demon cursed to have reach that spanned miles.</gloss></term> (76) attacks Rāma; his long arms are hewn off (77), *whereupon he becomes a deity again* (78). Explanations (79-85) and good counsel: go to Sugrīva on Mt. Ṛṣyamūka (86), who is wise but sad (87) because of what happened to him (88). Advice continues (89-94). Their ways part (95). Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa in the forest (96) meet a <term>Sabarī<gloss>A female forest-dweller and devotee.</gloss></term>-hermit (97) of excellent behavior (98-99), whom Rāma addresses (100) *by interrogating and preaching* (101-103). She replies (104) *by telling of her previous repulsive action* (105-110), is cured by Rāma (111), and recommends gaining Sugrīva's friendship, which will lead to good results (112-113).

Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa go to the Pratita-kampa forest (114), which is *very lovely*; *so is its lake* (115). Rāma is sad (116); he bewails his sad fate amidst the lovely nature (117-128). Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa go on to the frightful Mount Ṛṣyamūka (129).

Sugrīva is severely afflicted (130), goes to Mount Malaya (131), and sends Hanumān to Rāma (132). *Hanumān, appearing as a flying giant* (133), approaches Rāma as a <term>mahātma<gloss>original: mahātma; a "great soul" or spiritually enlightened person.</gloss></term> (134), and tells about the mountain's horrors (135-138) and dangers (139). Rāma introduces himself (140-141), tells about Sutiḳṣṇa (142) and Sītā's abduction (143). Hanumān tells of Sugrīva's sad fate (144-147). Rāma accompanies Hanumān (148) and arrives at Mount Malaya (149). Friendship is established between Rāma and Sugrīva (150-151). Sugrīva relates Wālin's valor (152-156). Rāma shows his dexterity (157). Sugrīva and Rāma go toward Mount Kiṣkindha (158) to kill Wālin (159).

*A huge cave is Wālin's residence* (160). Sugrīva challenges Wālin (161) and the two monkey-kings struggle fiercely (162-164); *the yogis are astonished* (165). Rāma cannot distinguish who is who (166); Sugrīva recoils (167) and blames Rāma (168). *Rāma exculpates himself* (169); he finds a stratagem (170) and kills Wālin (171).

*Wālin is mortally wounded* (172) and scolds Rāma (173-176). *Wālin acknowledges his misbehavior and abuses Rāma* (177-181) with arguments (182-184). Rāma exonerates himself (185) *with argument* (186), at some length (187-189). Wālin is ashamed (190); *adoration of Rāma by Wālin* (191); Wālin takes leave of Sugrīva (192), hopes for the future (193), and exculpates himself (194-195).

*Wālin dies and is honored* (196); Sugrīva accepts homage (197).

<summary>This page outlines the transition from Rāma's grief over Sītā's abduction to his alliance with the monkey king Sugrīva. It covers the death of the vulture Jaṭāyu, the liberation of the demon Dīrghabāhu, and the eventual duel between the monkey brothers Sugrīva and Wālin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the structural summary of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa. Following the aftermath of the battle in the forest and the death of the vulture Jaṭāyu, the narrative moves into the Kiṣkindha-kāṇḍa, focusing on the alliance between Rāma and the monkey kingdom, the onset of the rainy season, and the eventual search for the abducted Sītā.</meta>

| Verse Numbers | Meter Name | Summary of Content |
|:---|:---|:---|
| 198 | <term>upendra-vajra<gloss>The Lesser Thunderbolt of Indra meter</gloss></term> | *Aṅgada is appointed as a <term>yuva-rāja<gloss>crown prince or heir apparent</gloss></term>; the monkey warriors are honored with gifts.* |
| 199–200 | <term>citralekhā<gloss>The "Beautiful Portrait" or "Fine Writing" meter</gloss></term> | *The monkeys are content (199); Sugrīva pays his formal respects to Rāma (200).* |
| 201 | <term>dodhaka<gloss>The "Impelling" or "Shaker" meter</gloss></term> | *Rāma agrees to halt all military operations during the upcoming monsoon season.* |
| 202 | <term>tanu-madhyā<gloss>The "Slender-waisted" meter</gloss></term> | *Sugrīva asks for permission to depart.* |
| 203 | <term>mattamayūra<gloss>The "Drunken Peacock" meter</gloss></term> | *Sugrīva and his monkey host return to reign in Kiṣkindha <note>the capital of the monkey kingdom</note>. The rains begin.* |
| 7. 1–30 | <term>rathoddhata gati<gloss>The "Chariot-borne movement" meter</gloss></term> | *Sugrīva enjoys the pleasures of his kingdom while Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa retreat to Mount Malyawān (1). Rāma is moved by the beauty of the season (2). He observes the clouds and thunder (3); the rain feels like the arrows of Kāma <note>the God of Love</note> (4), the rainbow appears as Kāma’s bow (5), and the heron as his banner (6). As clouds cover the sky (7), Rāma weeps (8) and laments (9-11). He compares the firefly to his flickering hope (12), mentions the lightning (13) and thunder (14), and blames Kāma for his sorrow (15). He observes the <term>cātaka<gloss>a pied cuckoo bird said to live only on raindrops</gloss></term> (16), the wind (17), the peacock (18), and the falling rain (19). He sees holy seers (20), frogs, and birds (21). He addresses the absent Sītā with heavy sighs and many poetic comparisons (22-30).* |
| 31–42 | <term>pṛthvī<gloss>The "Earth" meter</gloss></term> | *Rāma’s miserable state continues (31). Autumn arrives (32). Rāma appeals to Lakṣmaṇa (33), noting that the dry season has come (34) but Sugrīva has become forgetful (35) and negligent of his promise (36). Rāma instructs Lakṣmaṇa to rebuke him (37), noting that Sugrīva is dazzled by his new power (38). Lakṣmaṇa travels to Kiṣkindha (39). Hanumān goes to meet him (40). Sugrīva apologizes for the delay (41) and promises to mobilize the monkeys (42).* |
| 43–54 | <term>śārdūla-vikrīḍita<gloss>The "Leap of the Tiger" meter</gloss></term> | *The mobilization begins (43). Sugrīva arrives at Malyawān (44) and confesses his grave negligence (45-46); Rāma is satisfied (47). Sugrīva gives a stirring speech to the monkey chiefs (48-51). Rāma sends Hanumān as his primary messenger (52). Hanumān departs (53). Satabali is sent to the North, Susena to the West, and Winata to the East (54).* |
| 55–70 | <term>tvarita-gati<gloss>The "Swift-paced" meter</gloss></term> | *Hanumān travels South (55) and reaches the Windhya mountains (56), facing many difficulties (57). Lions are frightened by the host (58); the monkeys grow weary (59) and stop (60) to rest on flat stones (61). They observe the birds (62) and enter a mysterious cave (63). Inside, they find a white building (64) and a beautiful young woman guarding it (65), who welcomes them (66). She questions them; they explain their presence (67) and their mission (68), informing her of the current situation regarding Rāma and Sītā (69-70).* |
| 71 | <term>tanu-madhyā<gloss>The "Slender-waisted" meter</gloss></term> | *The "She-with-the-slender-waist" <note>referring to the guardian woman</note> answers them (71):* |
| 72–75 | <term>āryā<gloss>The "Noble" meter</gloss></term> | *“The <term>Dānawa<gloss>a race of demons or giants</gloss></term> king Wiśwakarma <note>the divine architect</note> created this house and cave (72). Indra killed him (73). I am Swayamprabhā, the daughter of the <term>Dānawa<gloss>demon</gloss></term> king Merusāwarṇi (74). You must cover your eyes (75).”* |
| 76–77 | <term>upajāti<gloss>The "Composite" or "Hybrid" meter</gloss></term> | *She deceives the monkeys (76). It is noted that the <term>Dānawas<gloss>giants</gloss></term> and <term>rākṣasas<gloss>demons</gloss></term> help one another (77).* |
| 78–110 | <term>vaṃśastha<gloss>The "Lineage" or "Totem" meter</gloss></term> | *The monkeys are tricked (78). They fall into a deep sleep (79) and lose a full month of time (80). Upon waking, the monkeys are despairing (81-84). The giant bird Sampāti approaches them (85). The monkeys are terrified (86) and lament their fate (87-89), even hoping to be eaten to end their failure (90-91). However, Sampāti reveals he is the elder brother of Jaṭāyu and encourages them (92-99) by prophesying the future (100-105). The monkeys travel to Mount Mahendra (106), where they can see the Southern Sea (107) with its massive waves (108), hidden treasures (109), and powerful winds (110).* |

It details the monkeys' encounter with the ascetic Swayamprabhā and their meeting with the vulture Sampāti at the edge of the southern ocean.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the summary of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, transitioning from the end of the seventh section into the eighth. It tracks the narrative through the shifting poetic meters, focusing on Hanuman's journey to the island of Laṅkā and his first encounter with the captive Princess Sītā.</meta>

| Verse Numbers | Meter Name | Summary of Content |
|:---|:---|:---|
| 111–2 | <term>kusuma-vicitra<gloss>The "Variegated Flowers" meter</gloss></term> | The monkeys descend (111) and behold the fishes and crabs (112). |
| 113 | <term>pṛthvī<gloss>The "Earth" meter</gloss></term> | Aṅgada <note>the monkey prince</note> gives orders to start the mission; Hanuman prepares himself for the great leap. |
| **8.** 1–7 | <term>druta-vilambita<gloss>The "Fast-Slow" meter</gloss></term> | Hanuman in his flight (1-4) is swallowed by the demoness Ḍākiṇī (5); Ḍākiṇī is killed (6), and her corpse is devoured by sharks (7). |
| 8–17 | <term>praharṣiṇī<gloss>The "Joyful" meter</gloss></term> | Mount Menakā is seen rising from the sea (8). Mount Menakā invites Hanuman to rest (9-14), but he refuses (15-17). |
| 18–58 | <term>pramitâkṣarā<gloss>The "Measured Syllables" meter</gloss></term> | The demoness Wikatâkṣiṇī appears (18), *swallows Hanuman (19) and chokes (20)*; Hanuman kills her (21). Hanuman arrives at Mount Suwela (22) and is very cautious (23). Hanuman finds the enemy fortifications (24). Night falls (25). Hanuman keeps himself hidden (26). The <term>rākṣasas<gloss>demonic warriors</gloss></term> chat (27) and amuse themselves (28), *muttering formulas to induce sleep* (29), *practicing yoga* (30), *discussing the <term>śāstras<gloss>sacred legal and scientific texts</gloss></term>* (31), *eating human flesh* (32-3), *drinking fat and blood* (34), and *dancing in their drunkenness* (35). Others are flying through the air (36). Some boast of their exploits (37); there is no lack of girls (38). Hanuman is sad (39), and his grief increases (40). The moon rises (41) at the mountain of the dawn, Udaya-giri (42); Hanuman sees a temple (43), which is described in great detail (44-58). |
| 59–91 | <term>śālinī<gloss>The "Graceful" meter</gloss></term> | Hanuman sees the <term>rākṣasas<gloss>demonic beings</gloss></term> (59-61), *amusing themselves* (62-6), and drinking (67). Hanuman searches every house (68) while disguised in the shape of a <term>rākṣasa<gloss>demon</gloss></term> (69). He goes to Rāwaṇa’s palace (70), the abode of Rāwaṇa’s wives (71), where Kāma <note>the God of Love</note> reigns (72). He sees the celestial chariot Puṣpaka (73) and eventually finds (74) Rāwaṇa (75-78). Hanuman is disappointed (79) and bewildered (80). Taking the form of a monkey again (81), he looks for Sītā, whom he cannot find (82-84). Hanuman sees an <term>aśoka<gloss>a sacred flowering tree</gloss></term> grove (85) to the East of Laṅkā (86); it is a luscious grove (87) where even the moon seems to stop (88). There are nymphs (89), demonesses (90), and the poor, suffering Sītā (91). |
| 92–170 | <term>punarmada<gloss>The "Renewed Delight" meter</gloss></term> | Hanuman is careful (92); early morning arrives (93). Hanuman remains cautious (94). A description of the <term>aśoka<gloss>flowering tree</gloss></term> grove is given (95-97). Sītā appears cheerful for a moment (98). *Hanuman is glad (99). Music sounds in the grove (100). The moon turns pale as day breaks (101).* Hanuman, being cautious (102), watches Sītā (103) and recognizes her (104). Sītā is very thin (105), her hair is disheveled (106), and she is lying on the bare floor (107), teased by the demonesses (108-110). Daybreak comes (111). Rāwaṇa comes to the grove (112), woos Sītā (113-114), and offers her jewels (115), but Sītā is steadfast in her devotion to Rāma (116). Rāwaṇa becomes angry (117). Rāwaṇa woos Sītā again (118-126), but Sītā only praises Rāma’s superiority (127-134). Rāwaṇa menaces Sītā with a dagger (136) and harsh words (137), then retires (138). The demonesses threaten Sītā (139); only Trijaṭā, the daughter of Wibhiṣaṇa <note>Rāwaṇa’s righteous brother</note> (140), defends Sītā (141-142). The demonesses retire (143). *Sītā speaks to Trijaṭā about her frightful plight* (143-170). |
| 171–6 | <term>turagagati<gloss>The "Horse’s Gait" meter</gloss></term> | *Sītā bewails her unfortunate state.* |
| 177 | <term>mālinī<gloss>The "Garlanded Woman" meter</gloss></term> | *Sītā and Trijaṭā go to the temple to invoke the gods’ help.* |
| 178–91 | <term>punarmada<gloss>The "Renewed Delight" meter</gloss></term> | Hanuman is satisfied with what he has seen (178). He tells Sītā about Rāma’s sorrow (179). Sītā is suspicious of him at first (180-186). Hanuman explains that Rāma sent him from Mount Malyawān (187-191). |
| 192–213 | <term>vaktra<gloss>The "Mouth" or "Face" meter</gloss></term> | Sugrīva <note>the Monkey King</note> is mentioned (192). Hanuman recounts his wanderings (193-194). He describes how Rāma has grown thin from grief and sent an envoy (195) bearing a ring (196). Lakṣmaṇa is mentioned (197), and the monkey army is ready (198). Hanuman praises Sītā’s cleverness in handling Rāwaṇa (199) and predicts Rāwaṇa’s eventual downfall. |

It details the various Sanskrit meters used in the Old Javanese Kakawin to heighten the emotional and descriptive weight of the narrative.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the detailed breakdown of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa summary, specifically detailing the climax of Hanumān’s mission in Laṅkā. It follows the narrative as Hanumān transitions from a secret messenger to a destructive force within King Rāwaṇa's capital.</meta>

| Verse Numbers | Meter Name | Summary of Content |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| [Continued from previous] | | Hanumān speaks of death (200) and urges Sītā <note>the captive princess</note> to give him a message for Rāma (201). | 215 | <term>aśva-lalita<gloss>The "Grace of a Horse" meter</gloss></term> | The devastation of the grove. |
| **9.** 1-26 | <term>kusuma-vicitra<gloss>The "Variegated Flowers" meter</gloss></term> | The devastation continues (1). Hanumān is angry (11). A struggle ensues between Hanumān and the <term>rākṣasas</term>, many of whom are frightfully wounded (12-26). |
| 27-29 | <term>toṭaka<gloss>The "Rod" meter</gloss></term> | Continuation of the frightful clash between Hanumān and the <term>rākṣasas</term>. |

| 42-50 | <term>citraraśmi<gloss>The "Bright Beam" meter</gloss></term> | Hanumān goes to the <term>pārijātas<gloss>celestial coral trees</gloss></term> (42), which are splendidly adorned (43), and other plants (44), which he ruins (45). Akṣa <note>Rāwaṇa's son</note> (46) attacks (47) and is defeated (48). He attacks once more (49), but is now killed (50). |
| 51-55 | <term>bhujangga-prayāta<gloss>The "Snake's Movement" meter</gloss></term> | Hanumān bathes in the ocean (51) and then continues the devastation (52-55). |
| 56-57 | <term>daṇḍaka<gloss>The "Staff" meter</gloss></term> | The sad plight of the wild animals chased from the <term>aśoka-grove</term>. |
| 58-84 | <term>bhujangga-prayāta<gloss>The "Snake's Movement" meter</gloss></term> | Hanumān waits for his opponents (58) in the hope of fighting Rāwaṇa (59). Indrajit <note>Rāwaṇa's eldest son</note> arrives (60) with a retinue (61) in an enormous chariot (62) at the grove (63). Hanumān challenges him (64). A frightful struggle occurs (65-72). Indrajit's horses are killed (73). Indrajit is astonished at his lack of results (74-80). Indrajit shoots the <term>nāgapāśa<gloss>the "serpent-noose," a magical weapon that binds the target with snakes</gloss></term> (81), which clusters around Hanumān (82). Hanumān falls (83). <note>Hanumān does not fall for lack of strength, but in order to be taken to see Rāwaṇa</note> (84). |
| 85-88 | <term>suvadanā<gloss>The "Beautiful Face" meter</gloss></term> | The <term>rākṣasas</term> are elated (85), and Indrajit is threatening (86). Hanumān is removed (87). The <term>rākṣasas</term> aim to kill the treacherous envoy, Hanumān (88). |
| 89-91 | unknown 4 | The <term>rākṣasas</term> bring Hanumān to court (89) and report (90). Rāwaṇa declares: “He must die” (91). |
| 92-93 | <term>halamukhī<gloss>The "Plough-faced" meter</gloss></term> | Wibhīṣaṇa <note>Rāwaṇa’s righteous brother</note> has compassion (92) and pleads for Hanumān with Rāwaṇa (93). |
| **10.** 1-9 | unknown 5 | Rāwaṇa argues that Hanumān should be killed. |
| 10-41 | <term>campaka-mālikā<gloss>The "Garland of Champaka Flowers" meter</gloss></term> | Hanumān extolls the virtues of Rāma (10-15), those of Lakṣmaṇa and Sugrīwa <note>the king of the monkeys</note> (16-22), and vilifies the slain enemies (23-25). Rāwaṇa argues again (26-32) at length (33-35), defending the slain <term>rākṣasas</term> (36-41). |
| 42-65 | <term>bhujaga-śiśusṛta<gloss>The "Movement of a Young Snake" meter</gloss></term> | Hanumān boasts of his exploits (42-46) and accuses Rāwaṇa (47-56). He again mentions the fallen foes Wirādha, Mārica, and Wālin (57-61). He makes insinuations against Rāwaṇa’s bad instigations (62-65). |
| 66-70 | <term>kāmadattā<gloss>The "Given by Desire" meter</gloss></term> | Hanumān says: “Return Sītā to Rāma” (66-68). Rāwaṇa angrily orders: “Ignite Hanumān’s tail” (69-70). |
| 71-72 | <term>nārāca<gloss>The "Iron Arrow" meter</gloss></term> | Hanumān’s tail is provided with highly inflammable material and set on fire. |
| **11.** 1-2 | <term>daṇḍaka<gloss>The "Staff" meter</gloss></term> | Hanumān sets the palace on fire; general chaos and panic. |
| 3-13 | <term>sragdharā<gloss>The "Bearer of a Garland" meter</gloss></term> | Laṅkā burns (3). Hanumān goes again to the <term>aśoka-grove</term> (4) to take leave of Sītā (5). His flight (6-8) and arrival back (9-12). He tells the monkeys that Sītā is alive (13). |

<summary>This page outlines the dramatic conflict in Laṅkā where Hanumān destroys the royal gardens, kills Prince Akṣa, and is eventually captured by Indrajit's magical serpent-noose. It concludes with Hanumān's defiant court appearance, his tail being set ablaze, and his subsequent burning of the city before returning to his allies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the structural summary of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, transitioning from Hanumān’s destructive mission in Laṅkā to his return to Rāma. It then outlines the preparations for the great war and provides a glimpse into the internal politics and court life within the demon kingdom of Laṅkā.</meta>

| Canto and Verse | Poetic Meter | Summary of Content | Reference |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **11.** 14–21 | <term>aupacchandasika<gloss>A meter often used for elegant descriptions</gloss></term> | Hanumān goes to the Windhya mountains (14). Rāma, Lakṣmaṇa, and Sugrīwa <note>the king of the monkeys</note> are on Mount Mālyawān (16), living as ascetics (17). Hanumān offers Rāma the <term>cūḍāmaṇi<gloss>a royal crest-jewel</gloss></term> and the letter from Sītā (19). Rāma is filled with joy (20). | |
| 22–36 | <term>śārdūla-vikrīḍita<gloss>The "Tiger's Sport" meter, used for powerful or emotional scenes</gloss></term> | Rāma opens and reads Sītā’s love-letter (21). Sītā reminds Rāma of their past and urges him to liberate her (22-32). Rāma laments (33-34). Hanumān and Lakṣmaṇa comfort him (35-36). | |
| 37–43 | <term>aupacchandasika</term> | Hanumān encourages Rāma and incites him to fight. | |
| 44–60 | <term>vaṃśastha<gloss>A stable, rhythmic meter</gloss></term> | Hanumān points to Sītā’s sad plight (44-46). Rāma is comforted (47-48) and suggests they begin their march (49). The monkey army travels to Mount Mahendra (50). A description of Mount Mahendra follows (51-60). | |
| 61–87 | <term>tāmarasa<gloss>The "Day-lotus" meter</gloss></term> | The army's descent to the ocean by day and under the moonlight. | |
| 88–94 | <term>praharṣiṇī<gloss>The "Gladdening" meter</gloss></term> | Rāma is very sad; he is comforted and encouraged by his brother Lakṣmaṇa. | |
| 95–96 | <term>mattā<gloss>The "Intoxicated" meter</gloss></term> | Rāma relaxes and falls asleep (95); the monkeys keep watch (96). | |
| **12.** 1–20 | <term>jaladharamālā<gloss>The "Line of Clouds" meter</gloss></term> | In Laṅkā, at the end of the night, the moon sets (1). The stars follow like loving wives (2); the girls and wives of the city awaken (3-20). | |
| 21–41 | <term>svāgatā<gloss>The "Welcome" meter</gloss></term> | The women are in different moods after a night of love; their lovers address them. | |
| 42–45 | <term>mattamayūra<gloss>The "Drunken Peacock" meter</gloss></term> | The lovers flatter the women and remind them of their amorous pastimes. | |
| 46–63 | <term>mālinī<gloss>The "Garlanded" meter</gloss></term> | A royal audience in Laṅkā; Patih <note>Prime Minister</note> Prahasta receives tokens of homage. | |
| 64–65 | <term>nārāca<gloss>The "Iron Arrow" meter</gloss></term> | Rāwaṇa <note>the demon king</note> appears; the Prime Minister and the <term>Senāpati<gloss>commander-in-chief</gloss></term> make their obeisance. <note>Note: There is no further comparison with the Bhaṭṭikāvya text from this point; the numbers on the right-hand side refer to Vālmīki’s Rāmāyaṇa.</note> | |
| **13.** 1–18 | <term>rucira<gloss>The "Bright/Beautiful" meter</gloss></term> | Wibhīṣaṇa <note>Rāwaṇa's righteous brother</note> prays to Saṅkara <note>the god Shiva</note> (1-2); he pays his daily visit to his mother (3-4). His mother asks him to admonish Rāwaṇa (5-13), which conforms to Wibhīṣaṇa’s own plans (14). Wibhīṣaṇa enters the court (15), is frightened to see Rāwaṇa's state (16-17), and salutes him (18). | |
| 19–97 | <term>vaṃśastha</term> | Rāwaṇa asks his Prime Minister’s advice (19-26); there is a general expression of loyalty (27-28). Prahasta dissuades them from mere talk now (29-30), as Rāwaṇa is so valiant (31-33) and Rāma, Lakṣmaṇa, and the monkeys are considered insignificant (34). He argues the war should be waged (35-38). Wibhīṣaṇa delivers a complete dissertation on <term>nīti-śāstra<gloss>the science of ethics and politics</gloss></term> and <term>artha-śāstra<gloss>the science of statecraft</gloss></term>, advising the restoration of Sītā to Rāma (39-97). | VI. 6–8<br>VI. 9 |
| **14.** 1–19 | <term>vasanta-tilaka<gloss>The "Ornament of Spring" meter</gloss></term> | Rāwaṇa’s maternal grandfather, Sumāli, gives the same advice as Wibhīṣaṇa. | |
| 20–63 | <term>turagagati<gloss>The "Gait of a Horse" meter</gloss></term> | Kumbhakarṇa <note>Rāwaṇa's giant brother</note> awakes (20). He takes Wibhīṣaṇa’s side (22-23), but promises to help Rāwaṇa until inevitable death (24-33), then goes back to sleep (34). Wibhīṣaṇa describes bad omens and warns the king again (35-47). Rāwaṇa is furious at Wibhīṣaṇa (49-58), menaces him, and hits him (59). Wibhīṣaṇa remains silent and self-possessed (60-63). | VI. 12 |
| 64–69 | <term>śārdūla-vikrīḍita</term> | Wibhīṣaṇa states: “A king should dominate his passions.” | |

<meta>This page continues the structural analysis of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa. It lists the poetic meters used in Cantos 18 through 20 and summarizes the corresponding narrative events, which focus on the transition from espionage to the first major battles between Rāma's monkey army and Rāwaṇa’s demon forces.</meta>

| Canto and Verse | Poetic Meter | Summary of Content | Reference |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **18.** 1–16 | <term>aupacchandasika<gloss>a rhythmic meter often used for transitions</gloss></term> | Sukasāraṇa is sent by Rāwaṇa <note>the demon king of Laṅkā</note> to Mount Suwela to spy on the enemy; he is discovered by Wibhīṣaṇa and Rāma. Rāma sends him back to Laṅkā, where he reports his findings to Rāwaṇa. | <note>original: "VI. 25"; refers to the 25th chapter of the Book of War in the Vālmīki Rāmāyaṇa</note> |
| 17–21 | <term>aparavaktra<gloss>meaning "different-faced" or "uneven"</gloss></term> | Sukasāraṇa provides the first list of thirty monkey officers (see also Canto 19, verse 40 and Canto 22, verse 58). | <note>original: "VI. 26–8"</note> |
| 22–32 | <term>vaṅśastha<gloss>a meter meaning "staying in the lineage"</gloss></term> | Sukasāraṇa recommends that Rāwaṇa comply with Rāma's demands. Rāwaṇa becomes angry at this suggestion, but Sukasāraṇa remains loyal to his king. | <note>original: "VI. 29"</note> |
| 33–44 | <term>pṛthvī<gloss>meaning "the Earth"</gloss></term> | Rāma sends the monkey prince Aṅgada with an ultimatum (33–35); Aṅgada’s delivery of the message is quite provocative. | <note>original: "VI. 41"</note> |
| 45–52 | <term>śikharinī<gloss>meaning "the peaked one"</gloss></term> | Rāwaṇa refuses the ultimatum flatly; Aṅgada responds with threats, leaving Rāwaṇa impressed by his bravado. | |
| **19.** 1–4 | <term>praharaṇa-kalikā<gloss>meaning "the bud of a weapon"</gloss></term> | The monkeys and <term>rākṣasas<gloss>demons</gloss></term>, having eaten their fill, prepare themselves to wage war. | <note>original: "VI. 42?"</note> |
| 5–11 | <term>upajāti<gloss>a common Sanskrit meter formed by mixing Indra-vajrā and Upendra-vajrā</gloss></term> | Rāwaṇa plans to kill Sugrīwa <note>the king of the monkeys</note>; the text mentions his six councillors and twenty principal officers. | |
| 12–32 | <term>sragdharā<gloss>meaning "the garland-bearer," a long and grand meter</gloss></term> | The mobilization of the <term>rākṣasa<gloss>demon</gloss></term> warriors, who are shown taking leave of their wives before battle. | |
| 33–34 | <term>rajanī<gloss>meaning "the night"</gloss></term> | Prahasta, Ghaṭodara, Mahāpārśwa, and Indrajit are identified as the main demon officers. | |
| 35–38 | <term>śikharinī<gloss>the peaked meter</gloss></term> | Wirūpākṣa gives his orders; the <term>rākṣasas<gloss>demons</gloss></term> are boisterous and rowdy, even as evil omens begin to appear. | <note>original: "VI. 43?"</note> |
| 39–68 | <term>pṛthvītala<gloss>meaning "the surface of the Earth"</gloss></term> | A second list identifying thirty-five monkey officers is given. Mobilization begins. Lucky omens are seen (45–46). The monkeys flood the whole of Laṅkā (47–59). The <term>rākṣasas<gloss>demons</gloss></term> mobilize in <term>makarānana<gloss>a shark-mouthed battle formation</gloss></term> and <term>bajrapañjara<gloss>a "thunderbolt cage" formation</gloss></term>. | |
| 69–87 | <term>śārdūla-vikrīḍita<gloss>meaning "the tiger's play"</gloss></term> | The first clashes occur; the monkeys initially lose ground; Sugrīwa calls upon the generals Nala, Nīla, and Hanumān. | |
| 88–103 | <term>kusuma-vicitra<gloss>meaning "variegated with flowers"</gloss></term> | These three generals admonish the monkeys, who find fresh courage from their words. | |
| 104–112 | <term>śikharinī<gloss>the peaked meter</gloss></term> | The <term>rākṣasas<gloss>demons</gloss></term> are severely attacked and forced completely onto the defensive. | |
| 113–123 | <term>jaloddhata-gati<gloss>meaning "the movement of surging water"</gloss></term> | The war elephants, despite being armored, are left in a very poor state. | |
| 124–129 | <term>aśva-lalita<gloss>meaning "the graceful gait of a horse"</gloss></term> | More and more brave warriors from both the monkey and demon sides are killed. | |
| 130–131 | <term>rajanī<gloss>the night meter</gloss></term> | The battlefield is compared to a lake in the <term>Yama-loka<gloss>the realm of Yama, god of death</gloss></term>. | |
| **20.** 1–3 | unknown meter | A description of the horrific state of the battlefield. | |
| 4–6 | <term>mānavakā<gloss>meaning "the youth"</gloss></term> | The metaphorical lake of blood becomes dry; the exhausted heroes retire from the field. | |
| 7–14 | <term>śāliṇī<gloss>meaning "the stately one"</gloss></term> | The demon Prajaṅgha is defeated by the monkey Sampāti. | <note>original: "VI. 43"</note> |
| 15–19 | <term>pramitākṣara<gloss>meaning "measured syllables"</gloss></term> | The demons Sphuṭadākṣi and Pratapānākṣi are slain by the monkey Nala. | <note>original: "VI. 43"</note> |
| 20–24 | <term>sragdharā<gloss>the garland-bearer meter</gloss></term> | The demigods rejoice as they watch from the sky. Jambumālī is slain by Hanumān. | |
| 25–26 | <term>śārdūla-vikrīḍita<gloss>the tiger's play meter</gloss></term> | The demon Mitraghna is slain by Wibhīṣaṇa; Praghasa is slain by Sugrīwa. | <note>original: "VI. 43"</note> |
| 27 | <term>sragdharā<gloss>the garland-bearer meter</gloss></term> | The <term>Ṛṣis<gloss>sages</gloss></term> and <term>gandharwas<gloss>celestial musicians</gloss></term> are highly satisfied with the progress of the battle. | |
| 28 | <term>vasanta-tilaka<gloss>meaning "the ornament of spring"</gloss></term> | The praise of the <term>Ṛṣis<gloss>sages</gloss></term> encourages the warriors to fight harder. | |
| 29 | <term>sragdharā<gloss>the garland-bearer meter</gloss></term> | The demon Bajramuṣṭi is slain by the monkey Mainda. | <note>original: "VI. 43"</note> |

<summary>This page outlines the structure of Cantos 18-20 of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, detailing the transition from the failed espionage of Sukasāraṇa to the outbreak of the bloody war between the monkey army and the demon forces of Laṅkā.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, following the analysis of Canto 18 and the start of Canto 19. It details the escalation of the war in Laṅkā, specifically the use of magical weapons by Indrajit and the subsequent despair of Sītā and Rāma.</meta>

| Verse Number | Poetic Meter | Summary of Content | Reference |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 30–32 | <term>toṭaka<gloss>a rhythmic meter often used for energetic scenes</gloss></term> | The <term>rākṣasa<gloss>demon</gloss></term> Anikumbha is slain by the monkey commander Nīla. | <note>original: "VI. 43"; refers to Book 6, Chapter 43 of the Vālmīki Rāmāyaṇa</note> |

| Verse | Meter | Description |
|:---|:---|:---|
| 94–97 | <term>rathoddhata<gloss>meaning "borne by a chariot"</gloss></term> | Various plants are listed; their descriptions and unique characteristics. |
| 98 | <term>suvadanā<gloss>meaning "the lovely-faced woman"</gloss></term> | (Continuation of plant descriptions) |
| 99 | <term>vańśastha<gloss>a meter with a "steady lineage" rhythm</gloss></term> | (Continuation of plant descriptions) |
| 100–103 | <term>aparavaktra<gloss>meaning "the other face"</gloss></term> | (Continuation of plant descriptions) |
| 104–105 | <term>svāgatā<gloss>meaning "welcome"</gloss></term> | (Continuation of plant descriptions) |
| 106–107 | <term>pramitākṣara<gloss>meaning "measured syllables"</gloss></term> | Praise for the River Sarayū, the source of life for the city of Ayodhyā. |
| 108 | <term>rajanī<gloss>meaning "night"</gloss></term> | Description of the methods used for catching shrimp in the River Sarayū. |
| 109–117 | <term>aparavaktra<gloss>an uneven meter</gloss></term> | The cattle and their conversation (continuing the wordplay involving *sapi* <note>original: "sapi," the Old Javanese word for cow, used here for poetic puns</note>). |
| **26.** 1 | <term>suvadanā</term> | Description of the ancient city of Ayodhyā and the constant bringing of offerings. |
| 2–3 | <term>śārdūla-vikrīḍita<gloss>meaning "the tiger’s play"</gloss></term> | The great commotion among the people at the sight of the *Puṣpaka* <note>the celestial flying chariot</note>. |
| 4–5 | <term>kusuma-vicitra<gloss>meaning "strange flower"</gloss></term> | Description of the appearance of the landscape below. |
| 6 | <term>puṣpitâgrā<gloss>meaning "flowering at the tip"</gloss></term> | Description of the people in that same place. |
| 7–8 | <term>mālinī<gloss>meaning "the flower-girl"</gloss></term> | Bharata <note>Rāma’s brother</note> comes to meet them; Hanumān also arrives, as does the message: "My mother Kauśalyā..." (Compare to Vālmīki Rāmāyaṇa Book VI, 125). |
| 9–21 | <term>druta-vilambita<gloss>meaning "fast-slow"</gloss></term> | Rāma honors his mother Kauśalyā, and greets Bharata and Kekayī <note>Kaikeyī</note> in a mixture of joy and sorrow. Wibhiṣaṇa and Hanumān are introduced to Kauśalyā; Bharata pays his respects to Rāma. An audience is held for the mutual recognition of relatives and friends. |
| 22 | <term>daṇḍaka<gloss>a long, rhythmic meter used for elaborate descriptions</gloss></term> | Offering of presents; general rejoicing and relaxation. |
| 23 | <term>daṇḍaka</term> | The monkeys reveling in drink. Sugrīwa <note>the monkey king</note> gives presents; further festivities. |
| 24 | <term>daṇḍaka</term> | A banquet in the hall for the royalty; the common people feast in the open air. |
| 25 | <term>daṇḍaka</term> | Description of the royal banquet, narrated in part by the clown Bhandira <note>a comic character unique to the Javanese tradition</note>. |
| 26–35 | <term>vasanta-tilaka<gloss>meaning "the ornament of spring"</gloss></term> | The marital delights of the reunited Rāma and Sītā on the night of the 4th lunar day. |
| 36 | <term>vańśapatra-patita<gloss>meaning "fallen bamboo leaf"</gloss></term> | The royal banquet lasts a full week, ending on the 10th day. |
| 37 | <term>mālinī</term> | The guests are bestowed with gifts upon their departure and return home. |
| 38 | <term>śārdūla-vikrīḍita</term> | Trijaṭā <note>the niece of Rāwaṇa who befriended Sītā in captivity</note> takes leave of Sītā and is richly rewarded. |
| 39–40 | <term>dodhaka<gloss>meaning "the milker"</gloss></term> | Sītā directs her final words to her former maid and guardian Trijaṭā. |
| 41–42 | <term>praharṣiṇī<gloss>meaning "she who gladdens"</gloss></term> | May Trijaṭā's past prayers for the sake of Rāma and Sītā be heard. |
| 43–44 | <term>turagagati<gloss>meaning "the gait of a horse"</gloss></term> | As an act of merit, Trijaṭā must liberate innumerable geese and buffaloes. |
| 45–47 | <term>svāgatā</term> | Sītā is grateful and flattered; Trijaṭā, Wibhiṣaṇa, and Hanumān return to their own countries. |
| 48 | <term>aupacchandasika<gloss>a meter based on the vaitālīya form</gloss></term> | Rāma remains in Ayodhyā, protecting the land. |
| 49 | <term>aśvalalita<gloss>meaning "the play of a horse"</gloss></term> | Hail to Paramêśwara <note>The Supreme Lord, Shiva</note>, the Lord of the Three Worlds. |
| 50–1 | <term>wohiṅrat<gloss>an Old Javanese meter, likely meaning "fruit of the world"</gloss></term> | The Rāmāyaṇa brings good fortune; Yogīśvara <note>the traditional name for the poet</note> is a noble poet. |
| 52 | unknown 10 | The poet asks for forgiveness <note>a traditional closing trope in Javanese literature known as the *kṣamā*</note>. |

# APPENDIX IV

## Demons & Monkeys.
<note>original: "Rākṣasas & Monkeys"</note>

Comparing the destinies of dozens of demons <note>original: "rākṣasas"</note> and monkeys <note>The Vānaras, the magical forest-dwellers who served as Rāma's army</note> might provide a clue regarding the relationship between the different versions <note>original: "recensions"</note> of the story in which they appear. It seemed outside the scope of this study to produce complete lists of them; therefore, only those who meet an extraordinary or unusual end are included here.

### A. I. Male Demons.
<note>original: "Male Rākṣasas"</note>

Demon chiefs are mentioned in Vālmīki’s Book VI, chapters 9, 19, 36, 37, 43, 52-58, 59, 68-71, 75, 76, and 89.

1. *Triśiras* <note>meaning "Three-Headed"</note> is killed in the early conflict caused by Śūrpaṇakhā <note>Rāvaṇa's sister; her name means "she with claws like winnowing fans"</note> (Vālmīki III. 27; Bhaṭṭi’s Poem IV. 45; Javanese Rāmāyaṇa IV. 76). However, he appears again on the battlefield without further explanation and is killed a second time (Vālmīki VI. 70; Bhaṭṭi’s Poem XV. 84; Javanese Rāmāyaṇa XXIII. 16).
2. *Nikumbha* <note>meaning "Pot-hand"</note> is killed (Vālmīki VI. 43; Bhaṭṭi’s Poem XIV. 34; Javanese Rāmāyaṇa XX. 32), then is shown by Vibhīṣaṇa <note>Rāvaṇa's brother who defected to Rāma; his name means "The Terrifying"</note> to Rāma as one of his principal remaining adversaries (Vālmīki VI. 59) and is subsequently killed again (Vālmīki VI. 77; Bhaṭṭi’s Poem XV. 122; Javanese Rāmāyaṇa XXIII. 47).
3. *Prajaṅgha* <note>meaning "Long-shanks"</note> is slain (Vālmīki VI. 43; Bhaṭṭi’s Poem XIV. 31; Javanese Rāmāyaṇa XX. 14), then listed as one of Rāvaṇa’s last six principal chiefs (Vālmīki VI. 75) and is killed a second time (Vālmīki VI. 76; Bhaṭṭi’s Poem XV. 116; Javanese Rāmāyaṇa XXIII. 41).
4. *Ākampana* <note>meaning "The Trembler"</note> is slain (Vālmīki VI. 56; Bhaṭṭi’s Poem XIV. 86; Javanese Rāmāyaṇa XXI. 197), then shown by Vibhīṣaṇa to Rāma as one of his principal remaining adversaries (Vālmīki VI. 59). Vālmīki mentions him no more, though in Book VI. 76 he records a certain *Kampana* <note>meaning "Quivering"</note> being slain, but not

<summary>This page analyzes discrepancies in the Rāmāyaṇa narrative where certain demon characters are killed twice, suggesting different textual traditions were merged or poorly reconciled. It includes a detailed concordance table linking verses from the Sanskrit originals to the Old Javanese adaptation.</summary>
<keywords>Triśiras, Nikumbha, Prajaṅgha, Ākampana, Rākṣasa, textual criticism, concordance, comparative literature, Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, Vālmīki, Bhaṭṭikāvya</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison of various versions of the Rāmāyaṇa epic, specifically examining inconsistencies in how the deaths of demon generals are recorded across the Sanskrit Vālmīki text, the Sanskrit Bhaṭṭikāvya (BhK), and the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa (OJR).</meta>

mentioned before; <term>BhK</term> <note>The Bhaṭṭikāvya, a 7th-century Sanskrit poem that also serves as a grammar textbook</note> XV. 115 and <term>OJR</term> <note>The Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, the oldest and most famous epic poem in Old Javanese literature</note> XXIII. 41 kill **Kampana** in the same stanza; they also fail to mention him before, and include **Akampana** for a second time.
5. **Narântaka** dies only once in BhK XV. 79 and OJR XXIII. 12; however, he dies twice in the Vālmīki version (VI. 58 and 69). In between those accounts (VI. 59), Vibhīṣaṇa points him out to Rāma as one of the major enemies still remaining.
6. **Virūpâkṣa** dies only once definitively in Vālmīki VI. 43, but dies twice in both the BhK (XIV. 35 + XVII. 78) and the OJR (XX. 34 + XXIII. 83).
7. **Dhūmrâkṣa** is slain once (VI. 52; BhK XIV. 81; OJR XXI. 190) and then once more only in OJR XXIII. 84, where he seems to have been confused with the character in entry number 8.
8. **Yupâkṣa**, slain in Vālmīki VI. 76, dies twice in BhK (XV. 116 + XVII. 79). He is mentioned in OJR XIX. 8, but his death is not recorded at the appropriate time.

The conclusions to be drawn from these eight irregularities are as follows: Points 1 through 3 prove the high authority of the Vālmīki text and the early period in which additions were made—assuming the BhK is based on Vālmīki. Points 4, 7, and 8 show confusions that do not allow for firm conclusions: OJR XIX. 41 mentions **Akrandana** as a monkey, while XX. 59 and XXIV. 244 mention **Krandana**; clearly, the same <term>demon</term> <note>original: "rākṣasa"</note> or monkey must be intended. Points 5 and 6 show consistent irregularities between the BhK and OJR, which both differ from the Vālmīki text.

Vālmīki features the duplicate pair of characters Suka and Sāraṇa (VI. 27, 28). <term>Yogīśvara</term> <note>The traditional name attributed to the author of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</note> knows only one person, Sukasāraṇa. Bhaṭṭi skipped the episode of their spying and thus fails to provide a clue.

## A. II. Sītā’s demoness-friend.

Sītā’s <term>demoness</term> <note>original: "rākṣasī"</note> helper in Vālmīki V. 27/4 is called <note>original: "rākṣasī Trijatā vṛddhā" — the old demoness Trijatā</note>. In V. 37/11, the person described as <note>original: "jyeṣṭhā kanyā 'nalā nāma Vibhīṣaṇa-sutā" — the eldest daughter named Anala, daughter of Vibhīṣaṇa</note> must be the same demoness who is devoted to Sītā. In the translation by Makhan Lal Sen (published by Oriental Publishing Co., Calcutta, which is rather careless), we find her called: *Vibhīṣaṇa's eldest daughter, named Kala*. Vālmīki V. 58/83 mentions **Trijatā** again without feeling the need to re-introduce her. But in VI. 33/1, the text speaks <note>original German: "mir nichts dir nichts" — all of a sudden / out of the blue</note> about <note>original: "Saramā nāma rākṣasī" — a demoness named Saramā</note>, and this new name is mentioned again in VI. 34/5 and 20. In VI. 47/5 and 15, she is called **Trijatā** again; this name is maintained in 48/22, 39–40.

Hermann Jacobi, in his 1893 work *The Rāmāyaṇa* (page 133), mentions **Saramā** as the queen of Vibhīṣaṇa. He labels the episode in Book VI, chapters 33 and 34, as a "secondary addition," but he does not draw special attention to the other names.

BhK VIII. 99 and 101 corresponds to OJR VIII. 140 and 143, and Vālmīki V. 27, where **Trijatā** is the only one named, and her loyal services are mentioned. In the Old Javanese version, she plays a much more important role, always appearing as **Trijatā** (VIII. 140, 143, 145, 159; XVII. 61/2, 69, 78/9, 101/2, 116/7; XXI. 5, 7, 14, 36, 38, 41, 47/8, 55, 57; XXIV. 165, 168, 186/7; XXVI. 38–47) and she is ultimately rewarded for her kindness.

## B. Monkeys.

Vālmīki’s Rāmāyaṇa lists the demon officers in several places, but the principal monkeys are named even more frequently. Bhaṭṭi, on the other hand, provides no list of monkeys. As Yogīśvara tells us (XIX. 58), Bhaṭṭi viewed the monkeys as superficial and foolish; they cannot learn how to conjugate verbs, nor do they care about the past, present, future, or the <term>aorist</term> <note>A specific past tense in Sanskrit grammar; this is a joke referring to the Bhaṭṭikāvya's role as a grammar manual</note>. Bhaṭṭi mentions very few monkeys and invented only one new name: *Nir-ākula*, which means "Clear-Mind."

Yogīśvara, the Javanese author, liked the monkeys much better. There is little use in comparing his lists of monkeys (XVIII. 17–8; XIX. 40–1; XXIV. 243–52) with those in Vālmīki. However, from internal comparison, we see that XIX. 40–1 contains some unusual names:

<summary>This page analyzes the discrepancies in character names and death sequences between the Sanskrit and Old Javanese versions of the Rāmāyaṇa, specifically focusing on the demon generals and the identity of Sītā's guardian, Trijatā.</summary>
<keywords>comparative literature, demon generals, Trijatā, Saramā, monkey army, Sanskrit grammar, Yogīśvara, Bhaṭṭikāvya, Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa</keywords>

### Comparative Table of Sanskrit Court Poems (*Kāvyas*) and Old Javanese Epic Poems (*Kakawins*), showing the number of times they use a single-meter sequence of 2, 3, 4 etc.,<gloss>3</gloss> stanzas.

**Legend:**
**1** The notation "41 above 45," and similar entries, refers to the range 41–45. **2** Numbers within brackets<gloss>2</gloss> are those taken from cantos <note>original: "sargas"</note> where the poet makes a deliberate, virtuosic display of various meters. **3** This count excludes the mandatory single concluding stanza of a canto, as well as conclusions consisting of only 2 stanzas.
The order of the Sanskrit court poems follows Kühnau, *Journal of the German Oriental Society* <note>original: "ZDMG"</note>, 44, 1890; only the *Bhaṭṭi-kāvya* has been moved to appear closer to the Old Javanese *Rāmāyaṇa*. The order of the Old Javanese *Kakawins* follows the Balinese metrical manual <note>original: "Wawatěkan"</note>, see Krom in the *Journal of Indonesian Linguistics, Geography and Ethnology* <note>original: "TBG"</note> 57, 1916, pp. 508-21, with the insertion of the *Lineage of Hari* according to Friederich in the same journal.

# APPENDIX V

## *Key Markers for the study of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa.*

This last Appendix is mainly intended for those in Leiden, Jakarta, Singaraja and elsewhere, who have easy access to manuscripts <note>original: "MSS"</note> of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa <note>original: "OJR"</note>, the most massive poem of its genre in both India and Indonesia.

The Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa was held in such high esteem—and with good reason! <note>original: "et pour cause!"</note>—in Bali, that the "Variant Readings" <note>original: "Verschil van Lezingen"</note> in Kern’s edition—based upon some 7 manuscripts—took only 18 pages in comparison with 316 of the text itself. Now Kern, as early as 1900, did not bother his readers or himself by burdening his variant readings <note>original: "variae lectiones"</note> with obvious minor mistakes by copyists; even if he had done so, it would still have been apparent that the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa is an essentially very well-preserved text. Still, it offers perhaps in one or two of its copies a clue to the problems raised in this paper, and it might prove useful to scrutinize every manuscript on the following points:

1. Does every copy indeed contain 26 cantos <note>original: "sargas"</note>?
2. If not, then are perhaps cantos IX and X, or XIII and XIV, or XV and XVI taken together in one canto?
3. Does every manuscript end canto V and begin canto VI as in Kern’s edition?
4. Does any manuscript contain a stanza VIII. 135 or stanzas XXI. 93-7?
5. Does every manuscript in XV. 27 and XVI 24-9 read exactly the same as in the printed edition, or do they support the modest and clever <note>original: "astucious"</note> emendations proposed by Aichele in his "Fundamentals of Kawi Interpretation" <note>original: "Grundsaetzliches zur Kawi-Interpretation"</note>, *FBG* I, p. 18-9, and his "The Form of Kawi Poetry" <note>original: "Die Form der Kawi-Dichtung"</note>, *OLZ* 29, 1926, p. 933-9 (or in my translation "The Form of Kawi Poetry" <note>original: "De Vorm der Kawi-poezie"</note>, in *DJAWA* 11, 1931, p. 174-80)?
6. After finishing this study I examined the 80 poetic chimes <note>original: "yamakas," a complex form of Sanskrit-style wordplay</note> in cantos XVI, XVII, XIX, XXI, XXIII-XXVI. As a result I feel uncertain about the present state of tradition in XVI. 31; XVII. 128, 131; XIX. 35, 114, 122.

<summary>This page lists specific linguistic and structural "shibboleths"—identifying markers—that scholars should look for in Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa manuscripts to determine their authenticity and lineage. The author expresses doubt about certain passages where names like Sītā appear in non-traditional forms like 'Sinta'.</summary>
<keywords>Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, philology, manuscript studies, Hendrik Kern, Poerbatjaraka, sargas, yamakas, Kawi poetry, text criticism</keywords>

Kṛṣṇa, Draviḍa, Pralambôdara, Visaṅkaṭa, Siṁhânana; and also the passage XXIV. 243-52; Akampya, Kāma-dṛṣṭ, Puṇyavān, Mahābāhu. Keśara instead of <note>original: "pro"</note> Keśarī, and above all Sinta instead of <note>original: "pro"</note> Sītā (XXIV. 251) make this last passage, even more than XIX. 40-1, rather suspect in my eyes. Kern and Juynboll <note>Hendrik Kern and H.H. Juynboll, pioneering 19th and early 20th-century Dutch scholars of Old Javanese</note> had no objections here; Poerbatjaraka <note>Raden Ngabehi Poerbatjaraka, a preeminent Javanese philologist who often challenged European interpretations</note> only stamps XXIV. 252 as ‘annoying’, giving no arguments.

# APPENDIX V

## *Key Markers <note>original: "Shibboleths"</note> for the study of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa.*

This last Appendix is mainly intended for those in Leiden, Jakarta, Singaraja and elsewhere, who have easy access to manuscripts <note>original: "MSS"</note> of the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa <note>original: "OJR"</note>, the most massive poem of its genre in both India and Indonesia.

The Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa was held in such high esteem—and with good reason! <note>original: "et pour cause!"</note>—in Bali, that the "Variant Readings" <note>original: "Verschil van Lezingen"</note> in Kern’s edition—based upon some 7 manuscripts—took only 18 pages in comparison with 316 of the text itself. Now Kern, as early as 1900, did not bother his readers or himself by burdening his variant readings <note>original: "variae lectiones"</note> with obvious minor mistakes by copyists; even if he had done so, it would still have been apparent that the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa is an essentially very well-preserved text. Still, it offers perhaps in one or two of its copies a clue to the problems raised in this paper, and it might...

<summary>This page presents the table of contents for a study on the Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa and begins Appendix V, which discusses the text's preservation and the reliability of previous scholarly editions like Kern's.</summary>
<keywords>Table of Contents, Old Javanese Rāmāyaṇa, Bhaṭṭi-kāvya, Kakawin, Hendrik Kern, Poerbatjaraka, philology, textual criticism, manuscript variants</keywords>
